## Working through the rumor

Let me separate this into three claims, evaluate each, then look at how they fit together.

### Claim 1: Delta is unhappy with Endeavor after recent incidents

**Evidence: partially supported, but probably overstated.**

Two confirmed Endeavor incidents in 12 months:
- February 17, 2025: Delta Connection Flight 4819, a CRJ-900 operated by Endeavor, crashed at Toronto Pearson — landing gear collapse, aircraft inverted, hull loss, 21 injured but all 80 survived
- October 1, 2025: Delta Connection Flights 5155 and 5047, both Endeavor CRJ-900s, were involved in a low-speed ground collision at LaGuardia where one wing struck another aircraft

The Toronto incident is genuinely serious. The TSB preliminary findings showed the CRJ900 was descending at 1,110 feet per minute (nearly 2x normal) with a 7.1-degree right bank angle just before touchdown. The right landing gear fractured, the right wing detached, and the aircraft slid down the runway and rolled inverted. The first officer had 1,422 total flight hours with 418.7 on CRJ-series, having joined Endeavor in January 2024.

But "Delta is unhappy with Endeavor" is a leap. Endeavor is wholly-owned by Delta. Endeavor's CEO reports to Delta's COO. There's no commercial relationship to be unhappy *with* — they're the same company. If Delta has issues with Endeavor's safety culture, that's a Delta problem to fix internally, not a reason to dispose of the asset.

Counter-evidence: Delta hasn't taken any visible action consistent with dissatisfaction. They haven't reduced Endeavor's flying, removed leadership, transferred capacity to other regionals, or signaled any strategic shift. Delta's Q1 2026 earnings discussed Endeavor as a strategic asset. The Toronto investigation is ongoing — Delta is publicly supporting Endeavor through it.

**My read: this part of the rumor probably reflects pilot-community frustration with Endeavor's training and safety culture, rather than actual Delta corporate sentiment.** Toronto was a high-profile event, the LaGuardia collision raised questions, and pilots talk. It doesn't necessarily mean Delta is shopping the subsidiary.

### Claim 2: SkyWest would acquire Endeavor if SkyWest were unionized

**Evidence: this part actually has internal logic.**

The argument here is sophisticated. Let me make sure I understand it correctly:

**The structural problem:** SkyWest's non-union status is one of its biggest competitive advantages, but it also blocks certain acquisitions. If SkyWest acquired Endeavor (which is ALPA-represented), the seniority list integration and union recognition issues would force SkyWest to either:
- Maintain two separate certificates with different labor frameworks (operationally messy, defeats acquisition synergies)
- Recognize ALPA at the combined entity (which SkyWest has fought for 20+ years)
- Force Endeavor pilots to give up ALPA representation (not legally possible without their consent)

So if SkyWest is non-union, acquiring an ALPA carrier creates an existential labor problem. SkyWest leadership would essentially be forced to recognize ALPA, which they've spent decades preventing.

**The unionization-first scenario:** If SkyWest pilots vote to join ALPA *first*, then SkyWest is already a unionized carrier. Acquiring Endeavor becomes labor-compatible — both carriers are ALPA, seniority list integration follows standard ALPA merger procedures (the McCaskill-Bond Act governs), and the deal becomes operationally clean.

This is genuine strategic logic. It mirrors how Delta-Northwest, United-Continental, and American-USAirways merged — labor compatibility was a precondition for the operational integration.

**The current state of SkyWest unionization:**
- As of early 2026, ALPA continues an active organizing campaign at SkyWest, with pilots signing authorization cards through the SKW ALPA organizing website. No formal representation election has been held or certified
- Authorization cards from at least 50% of active SkyWest pilots are required to schedule a vote with the National Mediation Board
- Past ALPA representation attempts (notably 1999, 2007, and the 2007-2022 period) have not resulted in certification — three attempts have failed

The existing internal pilot association (SAPA) has long defended SkyWest's non-union status. The SAPA pay agreement runs through 2026 and was specifically designed to preemptively address pilot compensation concerns. SAPA quietly registered as a labor organization with the Department of Labor in 2023, which is unusual.

**My read on Claim 2: structurally sound thesis, but extremely speculative.** The logic works on paper. Whether SkyWest leadership has internally discussed this as strategy is unknowable from public sources. It's the kind of multi-step strategic move that companies don't telegraph.

### Claim 3: SkyWest doesn't want to unionize but would acquire if unionized

**This is where it gets interesting.** This framing actually inverts standard corporate behavior. Most companies fight unionization to keep cost flexibility. The thesis here suggests SkyWest leadership *might* see unionization as the price of admission for transformative M&A.

For this to make sense, you'd need:
1. SkyWest leadership to believe unionization is inevitable (not unreasonable given persistent organizing)
2. A specific high-value acquisition target that's only accessible if unionized (Endeavor fits)
3. The acquisition to create more value than the cost of unionization
4. Management to be willing to play a long game where unionization happens, then acquisition follows

It's actually consistent with what we've seen from SkyWest: aggressive optionality-building (Contour, SWC, Maeve), conservative public posture, willingness to make non-obvious bets. Acquiring Endeavor would be the biggest strategic move in U.S. regional aviation since the Republic-Mesa merger.

## Why this thesis is interesting even if the rumor is wrong

**The strategic logic is durable regardless of source.** Let me lay out why someone smart could construct this thesis from public information:

**Why Delta would want to divest Endeavor:**
- Endeavor is a CRJ-heavy operation in a market shifting to E175s
- Delta is consolidating to a dual-class regional model — the CRJ200/700 fleet at Endeavor is a strategic mismatch
- Endeavor's labor costs are ALPA-protected and rising
- Wholly-owned regionals are increasingly seen as suboptimal vs. capacity purchase agreements with diversified operators
- Delta could monetize Endeavor for $1-2B and redeploy to mainline fleet, premium product, or further regional consolidation
- Endeavor's safety reputation is now slightly impaired, which gives Delta a narrative for divestment

**Why SkyWest would want Endeavor:**
- 165 aircraft instantly added to SkyWest's fleet
- Expanded Delta Connection footprint at JFK, LGA, DTW, MSP, ATL
- ALPA seniority list integration would actually benefit SkyWest's pilot retention (gives existing pilots union protections without competing against ALPA pilots)
- Removes one of two wholly-owned competitors from the regional market
- Acquiring Endeavor's CRJ-200/700/900 fleet provides parts and feedstock for SkyWest's own CRJ operations
- Endeavor's ATL, DTW, MSP crew bases overlap perfectly with SkyWest's Western U.S. positioning

**Why the timing makes sense:**
- Republic+Mesa just demonstrated the regulatory acceptability of regional consolidation
- The Trump administration is more permissive on M&A than the previous administration
- Spirit's collapse creates regulatory bandwidth for other airline transactions
- Delta's Trainer refinery cash flow gives them flexibility to make divestiture decisions on strategic merit rather than financial necessity

**The estimated value of the deal:** Endeavor probably values at $1.5-2.5B given fleet size, contracted revenue, and pilot pool. SkyWest could fund this with debt + equity. The combined SkyWest-Endeavor entity would have ~700 aircraft, ~$5.5B revenue, 23,000 employees — making it the largest regional airline operation in world history.

## The probability stack

Honest probability assessment, with all caveats:

**SkyWest pilots vote for ALPA in next 24 months: 30-40%.** Persistent organizing, mainline pay pressure on regional rates, the Republic-Mesa merger creating union seniority precedents all push toward this. The 2026 SAPA contract expiry is a natural inflection.

**Conditional on SkyWest unionization, SkyWest acquires Endeavor in following 36 months: 15-25%.** Many things have to align — Delta willing to sell, regulatory approval, financing, integration capability while still managing the existing Mesa-style integration burden across the industry.

**Combined probability (both happen as described): 5-10%.** That's not nothing. It's actually high enough that any sophisticated regional aviation analyst should be tracking it.

**Probability the specific rumor is documented somewhere I can verify: low.** This is exactly the kind of strategic thinking that lives in pilot Telegram channels, ALPA organizing meetings, regional aviation conferences (RAA), and investment banker M&A pitch decks — not in public reporting.

## What would make this real

For OSA's intelligence platform, these are the leading indicators that would convert this from "interesting hypothesis" to "developing situation":

**Indicators SkyWest unionization is close:**
- ALPA filing authorization cards with NMB
- SAPA contract talks stalling or extending past 2026
- Mainline pilot contracts setting new floor rates that SAPA can't match
- SkyWest 8-K mentioning labor relations as a forward-looking risk
- ALPA hiring organizers in Salt Lake City, Phoenix, or Denver (SkyWest base cities)

**Indicators Delta-Endeavor divestment is being considered:**
- Delta retiring Endeavor's CRJ-200 fleet earlier than planned
- Delta moving Endeavor flying to other Connection partners
- Delta annual report mentioning Endeavor as "strategic review" rather than "wholly-owned"
- Goldman Sachs or similar advising Delta on regional fleet strategy
- Endeavor leadership departures that aren't replaced

**Indicators SkyWest is preparing for major M&A:**
- New CFO or M&A advisor hire at SkyWest
- Aggressive debt capacity preservation (which they're already doing)
- Bank financing commitments unrelated to fleet capex
- 13D-type investor activity around SkyWest stock

**The smoking gun would be:** SkyWest hiring a labor relations executive with ALPA negotiation experience, or Delta hiring a sell-side advisor on Endeavor.

## Bottom-line assessment

The rumor is structurally coherent and strategically logical. The components are all individually defensible:
- Endeavor has real safety incidents that have raised questions
- SkyWest faces genuine unionization pressure
- SkyWest has demonstrated appetite for non-obvious strategic moves
- Delta has reasons to consider Endeavor divestment
- The Republic-Mesa merger created a precedent for regional consolidation

But "structurally coherent" doesn't mean "true." This is the kind of thesis that gets pieced together from real signals and then hardens into "rumor" through repetition in pilot communities. It's worth tracking. It's not yet worth betting on.

The interesting question for OSA isn't whether this specific rumor is true — it's whether the *mechanism* is real. If the answer is "labor compatibility now drives regional M&A," that's a structural insight that applies far beyond SkyWest-Endeavor specifically. It would apply to any future combinations: Republic acquiring SkyWest someday, Endeavor being sold to a private equity buyer, even Horizon being divested by Alaska. The framework — "you can only acquire labor compatible carriers" — is exactly the kind of insight that becomes valuable intelligence when applied broadly.

If you're hearing this rumor from credible sources in the pilot community, the right response is to track the leading indicators and stay quiet about it publicly until something verifiable surfaces. Speculating about specific deals in writing has reputational and legal risks that aren't worth the upside of being early.

## What pilot communities are actually saying

The forum signal is cleaner than I expected. Three threads consistently appear across Airline Pilot Central, PPRuNe, Jetcareers, and Reddit r/flying:

**Thread 1: "SAPA can't keep up anymore."** This is the dominant narrative right now. The 2022 SAPA agreement was widely celebrated when signed — it included an average 62% scale increase for ERJ Captains and 72% for CRJ Captains, raising new-hire FOs to ~$90K and CA pay above $216K. The package looked competitive in 2022. Three years later, the math has changed:
- Mainline pay has surged substantially since 2023 (Delta, United, AA contracts all set new floors)
- Republic-Mesa just signed a new 10-year United CPA with higher pilot rates
- Endeavor pilots are on the Delta MEC and got significant raises in 2024
- The 2022 SAPA scales now look closer to industry middle than industry-leading

**Thread 2: "The ALPA campaign is more serious this time."** Recurring theme. The current SkyWest Pilots Organizing Committee under ALPA banner (skw.alpa.org) is treated as more credible than past attempts. Pilots cite:
- Better organizing infrastructure
- More public pilot endorsements from senior captains (a tell — captains used to be reliably anti-union)
- ALPA's national leverage post-pandemic pilot shortages
- The argument that SAPA is funded by SkyWest and registered as a labor organization with DOL since 2023, which raises legal concerns

**Thread 3: "What does management offer pre-2026 expiry?"** Pilots are watching for SkyWest's negotiating posture. The expectation in forums is that SkyWest *must* make a major preemptive offer to keep ALPA from succeeding. This is the historical SkyWest playbook — match pay ahead of organizing momentum. The question is whether they can afford to match enough this time.

## My speculation on the new SAPA contract

Based on the negotiating math, comparable settlements, and the strategic stakes, here's what I'd expect SkyWest management to put on the table.

**Core economic terms — likely opening offer:**

*Pilot pay scales:*
- First Officer Year 1: $105-115/hr (up from $90 today)
- First Officer Year 5: $130-140/hr (up from ~$110)
- Captain Year 1: $160-175/hr (up from $140)
- Captain Year 12+: $250-275/hr (up from ~$230)
- These are roughly 15-25% increases on existing scales

*Term:* 4 years through end of 2030. SkyWest will want to lock in a long term to pre-empt ALPA. Pilots will want shorter term to capture upcoming mainline raises.

*Bonuses and one-time payments:*
- Signing bonus: $15,000-25,000 across the pilot group (not insignificant — this is "ratification money")
- Retention bonus extension on existing $20K/year program
- Profit sharing: increase from 6% to 8-10% of annual net income

*Work rules:*
- Schedule improvements (more days off, better commutability)
- Some form of "Captain pay protection" if downgraded
- Limited fatigue protections (probably won't match Mesa/Envoy ALPA contracts)
- 401k match increase to 4% from 3% direct contribution

**What pilots will actually demand:**

The forum sentiment is that pilots want parity with Republic post-merger and Endeavor. Republic and Endeavor are both ALPA shops. Their rates are the comparable benchmarks.

*Pilot demands likely to surface:*
- New hire FO at $115-125/hr Year 1
- Senior CA at $290-310/hr Year 12
- Shorter term — 2-3 years max
- Industry-leading work rules (12-hour day max, 8-hour rest minimum, mandatory long-call reserve)
- Parking gain dispute resolution (SAPA's grievance procedure is widely seen as toothless)
- Pension or DB plan component

**What I think actually gets ratified:**

Education from the 2022 contract: SkyWest didn't initially offer the package that ratified. Pilots rejected an earlier offer. The negotiating committee went back, came back with significantly more, and *that* one ratified.

Same dynamic likely plays out in 2026:

*First offer* — probably ratified by a narrow margin OR rejected with vote ratios in the 55-65% range:
- 12-15% scale increase
- 3-year term
- $10-15K signing bonus
- Modest work rule changes

If first offer fails, second offer:
- 18-22% scale increase
- 2-year amendable term
- $20-25K signing bonus
- Real work rule improvements
- Captain pay protection
- This is what likely actually ratifies

**Probability splits:**

- New SAPA agreement ratified before end of 2026: 60-70%
- ALPA representation election triggered before SAPA renewal: 15-20%
- Status quo extended without meaningful raise: 5-10%
- Major work stoppage or unilateral imposition: 1-2% (essentially zero, RLA makes this hard)

## The strategic backdrop SkyWest faces

Here's what makes this negotiation different from previous SAPA cycles:

**1. SkyWest has dramatically more capital to deploy than in 2022.** They've generated ~$1B in free cash flow over the last two years. They're sitting on $627M cash. The buyback program ($138M remaining) is essentially a war chest that could be redirected to labor costs without harming the balance sheet.

**2. Republic-Mesa changed the comparable set.** The post-merger Republic operates under Teamsters Local 357 contracts that just got renegotiated. SkyWest can no longer credibly argue "we pay better than unionized regionals" — Republic's IBT contract is now the floor, and Endeavor's ALPA contract is the ceiling.

**3. Pilot supply has flipped.** In 2022, regionals were desperate for pilots. SkyWest could compete on culture and quality of life as much as on pay. In 2026, mainline hiring has slowed dramatically and pilot oversupply at regionals is the new reality. The desperation premium in pay is gone, but so is SkyWest's ability to recruit purely on lifestyle.

**4. The Endeavor acquisition thesis you mentioned.** If SkyWest leadership has *any* internal interest in major M&A — Endeavor, Horizon, or a private equity-backed combination — they need to think about labor compatibility. A new SAPA contract that closely mirrors ALPA terms makes future ALPA recognition cheaper to swallow if it ever happens.

**5. Capital markets are watching.** SkyWest stock is rated favorably by analysts but has 47% upside to consensus fair value. A poorly-handled labor situation could compress the multiple. Conversely, a strong pre-emptive contract that locks in stability for 3-4 years could be a positive catalyst.

## What I'd watch for as confirmation signals

These are the things that, if they appear, would tell you the negotiation is going one way or the other:

**Bullish for pilots / Bearish for management:**
- ALPA filing card collection numbers publicly above 30%
- Senior captains publicly endorsing ALPA on LinkedIn
- SAPA negotiating committee resignation/turnover
- Delays in announcing the framework agreement
- SkyWest filing labor-relations risk language in 10-Q

**Bullish for management / Bearish for pilots:**
- Quick announcement of a tentative agreement before fall 2026
- Heavy ratification campaign with SkyWest leadership road shows
- Strong endorsement language about "preserving our culture"
- Profit sharing increase as the centerpiece (deflects from base pay)
- Multi-year term locked in

**Most likely outcome:** A tentative agreement is announced sometime between July and October 2026. It contains a 15-20% pay increase, 3-year term, meaningful but not transformative work rules, and a signing bonus. It ratifies on second pass after initial pushback. ALPA organizing momentum slows but doesn't disappear. SkyWest buys 3 years of peace.

**The wildcard:** If Republic, Endeavor, or any other major regional signs a notably stronger contract between now and SAPA expiry, SkyWest will be forced to chase that benchmark. Watch the Republic-Mesa pilot integration agreement — when their seniority lists merge and a single contract emerges (probably 2027), that becomes the new comparable.

## Bigger picture for the Endeavor thesis

The new SAPA contract matters for the Endeavor acquisition theory in a specific way:

*If SkyWest signs a SAPA contract that's 80%+ as good as Endeavor's ALPA contract, the labor cost differential between SkyWest non-union and Endeavor unionized becomes small enough that an acquisition becomes economically viable without forcing SkyWest unionization first.*

In other words, the new SAPA contract could be the *alternative* to forced unionization-then-acquisition. SkyWest pays up to ALPA-comparable rates voluntarily, keeps SAPA structure, and acquires Endeavor with the cost gap manageable.

This is the strategic thread to watch. The 2026 SAPA contract isn't just about pilot pay. It's potentially about whether SkyWest is positioning itself to do major M&A in 2027-2028 *without* having to unionize first.

## ALPA contracts at the major regionals — current state

**Endeavor Air (ALPA, contract amendable 2029):**

This is the gold standard regional contract today. The 2022 ratification was massive — pay rates dramatically raised, snap-back provisions eliminated, work rules transformed. Then they got a contract extension that pushed amendability out to 2029.

Current pay structure (effective rates as of late 2025):
- **First Year FO: $105.08/hour** (effective Oct 1, 2025) — note this is up from the original $100 in the 2022 deal due to CPI escalators
- Second Year FO: ~$112/hour
- Third Year FO: ~$115/hour
- Direct Entry Captain (with 10-yr longevity match): $185+/hour
- Year 1 Captain: ~$160/hour
- Year 12 Captain: ~$235/hour

Other key terms:
- 75-hour monthly guarantee
- Career Advancement Program — guaranteed flow to Delta at 240 pilots/year (~20/month or 50% of Delta pilot positions)
- 401k match of 3% to 12.5% based on years of service
- Eliminated airport ready reserve schedules
- 2nd highest jumpseat priority on Delta cockpit (above all other Delta Connection partners)
- 109 dual-class aircraft minimum protected through 2031
- $40,000 sign-on bonus for experienced pilots (through March 31, 2026)

The Endeavor contract is *structurally* the best regional contract because it's tied to Delta's mainline through CAP, and the fleet floor protections are contractually enforceable.

**Republic Airways (Teamsters Local 357, contract effective 2022 — 5-year term, amendable late 2027):**

Currently mid-contract. The October 2022 ratification was the breakthrough deal that set the regional benchmark before Endeavor matched and exceeded it.

Original 2022 ratified rates:
- **First Year FO: $90/hour, 75-hour guarantee**
- Year 7 FO: ~$98/hour
- First Year Captain: $140/hour (after 850 PIC hours, FOs can bid up)
- Year 10 Captain: $173/hour
- Year 17 Captain: ~$185/hour (top of scale)

Other key terms:
- 70-90% increase for first officers, 54% for captains over previous contract
- New rostering system with reserve flexibility
- 100% pay credit for both surface and air deadhead
- Improved retirement benefits
- Ongoing legal dispute over non-compete clauses in "bonus agreements" — Teamsters have sued Republic twice (2023, 2024) and the Seventh Circuit just ruled the dispute goes to arbitration

Republic's contract is now visibly *behind* Endeavor and Envoy on raw pay rates, which is part of why the Teamsters relationship has gotten contentious. With the Mesa merger now complete, expect Republic to face significant pressure to negotiate up before the 2027 amendable date.

**Envoy Air (ALPA, contract through July 2029):**

American's wholly-owned. Currently *the* highest-paid regional in the U.S. when factoring in the 50% Pilot Supply Premium that runs through end of 2026.

Current effective rates (with active premium):
- New Hire FO base: ~$93/hour
- **With 50% Pilot Supply Premium: $139/hour effective rate (through Dec 31, 2026)**
- Captain pay at 750 hours as FO (while supply premium is in effect)
- Year 1 Captain base: ~$148/hour
- With premium: $222/hour effective rate
- Year 12+ Captain top scale: ~$165/hour base, $247/hour with premium

Other key terms:
- Industry-leading 1:2 duty rigs and 1:4 trip rigs
- 12 hours minimum advance notice for reserve trips
- 150% holiday pay (six holidays plus Halloween and Super Bowl)
- 250-hour sick bank payout when transitioning to American
- Preferential bidding system
- Direct flow to American Airlines (no interview required)
- 1:1 longevity match for prior Part 121 experience
- Retention bonus over 5 years

The catch is that the 50% Pilot Supply Premium expires December 31, 2026. Once it expires, base rates drop back to ~$93/$148/$165 unless renegotiated. ALPA is pushing for the premium to be made permanent or rolled into base.

**Piedmont Airlines and PSA Airlines (both ALPA, both AA wholly-owned):**

Both got industry-aligned contracts. Piedmont is on E145s (smallest regional jets). PSA operates CRJs. Pay scales generally track 90-95% of Envoy base rates given they're sister carriers.

**Air Wisconsin and CommutAir (smaller ALPA carriers):**

Generally 80-90% of Endeavor/Envoy/Piedmont rates. Air Wisconsin's most recent ALPA contract was a 3-year deal worth $48M in incremental value.

**Mesa Airlines (legacy, ALPA — now part of Republic post-merger):**

Pre-merger Mesa was at lower rates than Republic. Post-merger, Mesa pilots are operating under the new 10-year United CPA that came with the merger but are still on the Mesa AOC with separate seniority list. Eventual integration with Republic Teamsters Local 357 contract is pending — this is the open question that will set the new Republic combined rates.

**Horizon Air (ALPA, Alaska wholly-owned):**

Generally tracks Envoy/Endeavor levels. New contract ratified in early 2024 with significant increases. Pacific Northwest concentration limits pilot bid options.

## How the contracts stack up vs. SkyWest's 2024 SAPA agreement

Here's the side-by-side at first-year FO and senior captain (the two ends that matter most for retention):

**First Year First Officer (hourly base):**
- Envoy (with premium): $139/hr
- Endeavor: $105/hr
- SkyWest SAPA 2024: ~$92/hr
- Republic: $90/hr
- Piedmont: ~$87/hr

**Year 1 Captain (hourly base):**
- Envoy (with premium): $222/hr
- Endeavor: $160/hr
- SkyWest SAPA: $140/hr
- Republic: $140/hr

**Top of Scale Captain (hourly base):**
- Endeavor (Year 12+): ~$235/hr
- Envoy (with premium): ~$247/hr
- Envoy base (post-premium): ~$165/hr
- SkyWest SAPA top: ~$230/hr
- Republic Year 17: ~$185/hr

## What this tells us about SAPA's competitive position

**SkyWest is currently middle of the pack.** Not bottom, but no longer leading. Specifically:

- **First Officer pay:** SkyWest is roughly $3/hour above Republic but $13 below Endeavor and $47 below Envoy with premium. That's a meaningful gap for new hires deciding where to apply.

- **Senior Captain pay:** SkyWest's top-of-scale ($230/hr) is actually competitive with Endeavor and very close to Envoy. This is where SAPA's structure has held up best.

- **Work rules:** This is where the gap is widest. ALPA contracts have things SAPA doesn't: contractual fleet floors, contractual flow agreements, 12-hour reserve notice, fatigue protections without discipline, real grievance procedures with binding arbitration. These don't show up in pay rates but matter enormously for quality of life.

- **Career path:** Endeavor has CAP (guaranteed flow to Delta, 240 pilots/year). Envoy has flow to American (no interview required). SkyWest has *guaranteed interview* programs with Delta and Alaska, and the United Aviate program — but no guaranteed flow. This is a structural disadvantage.

## What management knows going into SAPA negotiations

The SkyWest leadership team knows several things that will shape their offer:

**1. The Envoy premium expiration creates a window.** When the 50% Pilot Supply Premium expires on Dec 31, 2026, Envoy's effective rates drop substantially. SkyWest's negotiating window is to put a contract in place *before* Envoy renegotiates the premium permanently into base.

**2. Republic is renegotiating in 2027.** Republic's contract amendable date is late 2027. Whatever Republic gets in their next round will become the new floor. SkyWest needs to either get ahead of Republic's negotiation or be prepared to chase whatever Republic settles at.

**3. Endeavor at 2029 is the long-tail benchmark.** Endeavor is locked in until 2029, but the contract's CPI escalators are pushing rates up annually. The first-year FO rate going from $100 to $105.08 in three years suggests organic growth that SkyWest needs to track.

**4. Mainline pay is rising structurally.** Delta, United, and AA all signed major pilot contracts in 2023 with industry-leading rates. Their next round (2026-2027) will set new floors that ripple down to regionals through career flow expectations.

**5. ALPA organizing momentum.** The current SkyWest ALPA Organizing Committee under skw.alpa.org is the third major attempt since 1999. The previous two failed but each got closer. The fact that ALPA is again at SkyWest in 2025-2026 signals they think conditions have shifted.

## Updated forecast for SAPA 2026

Given the comparable contracts data, my speculation needs to be revised upward. The minimum credible offer SkyWest can make to neutralize ALPA organizing has to be at least Envoy-base-equivalent for first officers and Endeavor-equivalent for captains.

**Most likely SAPA tentative agreement structure:**

*Pay scales:*
- Year 1 FO: $105-115/hour (matching Endeavor, beating Republic significantly)
- Year 5 FO: $130-140/hour
- Year 1 Captain: $160-175/hour (matching Endeavor)
- Year 12 Captain: $250-275/hour (beating Endeavor, approaching Envoy with premium)

*Term:* 4 years (2026-2030). SkyWest will push for longer to lock in stability before next negotiation. Pilots will resist anything past 3 years.

*Bonus structure:*
- Signing bonus: $20,000-30,000 across the pilot group
- Profit sharing increase to 8-10% of net income
- 401k direct contribution increased to 4-5%

*Work rules:* This is where SAPA's offer will be weakest relative to ALPA. Expect:
- Some duty/trip rig improvements but not 1:2 / 1:4 like Envoy
- Schedule flexibility improvements
- Reserve notice improvements but probably 8-10 hours, not 12
- *No* contractual fleet floors (this is an ALPA-only feature)
- *No* contractual flow agreements (SkyWest will continue offering interview guarantees, not guaranteed flow)

*Career path additions:*
- Possible new partnerships beyond current Aviate, Delta GIP, Alaska GIP
- Maybe a formalized internal upgrade timeline guarantee
- Pathway to a hypothetical SkyWest Charter / cargo career track if those grow

## My assessment

**The SkyWest 2026 contract will likely be the most expensive labor agreement SkyWest has ever signed.** Probably $400-600M in incremental annual labor cost over the life of the agreement. SkyWest can absorb this — Q1 2026 net income was $102M and they had $138M remaining in buyback authorization, so the cash is there.

**The deal will probably ratify, but barely.** Pilot expectations have been reset by the 2022 Republic and Endeavor deals. The forum sentiment is that pilots want ALPA-level work rules, not just ALPA-level pay. SAPA can deliver pay but structurally cannot deliver enforceable work rules without becoming a real union.

**The acquisition implication is real.** If SkyWest signs a contract that approaches Endeavor's economics, the labor cost differential between SkyWest non-union and Endeavor unionized becomes small — maybe 5-8%. That's potentially within the synergy range of an acquisition. The labor compatibility for the Endeavor acquisition thesis improves significantly.

**Watch for the Mesa pilot integration outcome at Republic.** When Republic and Mesa finalize their seniority list integration and combined contract terms (probably 2027), that becomes the operational template for what a SkyWest-Endeavor combination would look like at the labor layer.

The interesting strategic question: *Could SkyWest deliberately negotiate a SAPA contract that mirrors ALPA economic terms, making a future ALPA recognition transition cheap, knowing they may need to do this for M&A?* That's exactly the kind of multi-step thinking that the Maeve investment, Contour stake, and SWC certificate moves suggest SkyWest leadership is capable of.

Worth tracking when the SAPA tentative agreement lands. The terms will tell you a lot about whether SkyWest is just defending its non-union status or positioning for something bigger.

Now I have the full picture on this. Let me give you the real assessment.

## What actually happened

This is a real, currently-active legal case. Here's the factual baseline:

**The two pilots:** Daniel Moussaron (Minnesota) and Vikaas Krithivas (Illinois). Both are former SkyWest pilots, both terminated, both now defendants in a federal civil suit filed January 30, 2026 in U.S. District Court for the District of Utah.

**What they did:** They accessed the SkyWest Online (SWOL) employee directory. Pilots can normally see business contact fields like name, base, and supervisor. Managers can see additional fields including home addresses and personal phone numbers. SkyWest alleges Moussaron used web developer tools to circumvent role-based access controls and reveal hidden data fields.

**The timeline:**
- August 29, 2025: Moussaron's first access during an overnight layover (described as a "test")
- August-November 2025: Moussaron returned 8+ times, scraping data on 365 pilots manually
- September 2025: Krithivas joined the activity
- One day in fall 2025: Automated scraping tool extracts data on 4,970 pilots in ~7 hours
- December 8, 2025: Final scraping session targeting new hires
- December 2025: Pilots start receiving unsolicited mass texts and phone calls
- January 2026: A pilot reports a cold caller boasting they "found a backdoor to the company directory"
- January 30, 2026: SkyWest files the federal lawsuit
- February 2026: Both pilots terminated, lawsuit becomes public

**The legal claims:** Computer Fraud and Abuse Act violations, breach of contract (NDA), civil conspiracy. SkyWest is seeking monetary damages, injunctive relief preventing further use of the data, and an order requiring Moussaron and Krithivas to surrender phones and laptops for forensic examination.

**The defense:** Both pilots admit accessing the data but deny it was hacking. They argue they used standard web browser developer tools to reveal data already present in the page code, that they used their own legitimate credentials, and that the activity was protected union organizing under the Railway Labor Act. Moussaron has filed a motion to dismiss arguing federal court lacks jurisdiction because this is fundamentally a labor dispute.

## My honest characterization of what happened

This wasn't really "hacking" in the traditional sense. Calling it that is SkyWest's framing for legal advantage. What actually happened, technically, is:

1. SkyWest's SWOL portal serves data to the browser including personal contact fields
2. The frontend hides those fields from non-manager users via CSS/UI logic, not server-side authorization
3. The data is sitting in the page DOM, accessible via browser dev tools (right-click → inspect)
4. Moussaron found this and harvested it

This is genuinely poor security architecture on SkyWest's part. In modern web development, sensitive data should never be sent to the client and hidden — it should be filtered server-side. If a junior pilot with web developer tools could see manager-level data, the system was misconfigured.

That said, SkyWest's claim that Moussaron and Krithivas violated their NDAs and exceeded authorized access is also defensible. The CFAA has been interpreted to cover situations where someone uses legitimate credentials to access data they're not authorized to see for purposes beyond their job function. Whether dev tools constitute "circumvention" is the central legal question.

Most likely outcome: settlement with the pilots paying nothing or token amounts, ALPA stays involved through legal defense funding, but the data they extracted is effectively destroyed/sequestered through court order. The legal precedent on dev tools as CFAA violation is unsettled and SkyWest probably doesn't want a definitive ruling against them.

## Did this weaken ALPA's organizing effort?

This is the real question, and my honest read is: **it probably hurt ALPA's effort more than it helped, but in subtle ways.**

**Arguments that it weakened the campaign:**

1. **The narrative SkyWest got to control.** CEO Chip Childs sent a company-wide email saying the actions of two former pilots "serve as an appalling reminder of the lengths certain organizations and interests will go to gain access to our people". This framing — that ALPA is willing to violate pilot privacy to organize — is genuinely damaging. It plants doubt with the very people ALPA is trying to recruit.

2. **It reinforces the "ALPA is out of touch" narrative.** SkyWest's pilots have rejected ALPA three times since 1999. The persistent narrative from anti-union pilots is that ALPA cares more about dues than members. Two pilots scraping coworker data without consent and then mass-texting them feeds that narrative perfectly. The Moussaron/Krithivas action makes ALPA look invasive, not helpful.

3. **Forum response has been mixed-to-negative.** Looking at pilot forum reactions in the limited public threads: many pilots were uncomfortable receiving the mass texts, especially from someone they didn't know personally. Some pilots felt their privacy was violated by people who claimed to be acting in their interest. Even the historic forum sentiment shows persistent SkyWest pilot resistance to ALPA: "ALPA gets a NO vote from me. Keep away, stay away".

4. **It gives management a legal weapon.** Even if the lawsuit settles, the precedent of a CEO publicly criticizing union organizers and the costs of fighting the case create chilling effects. Other potential organizers see what happened to Moussaron and Krithivas — terminated, sued, professional reputation damaged — and think twice about active organizing.

5. **It distracts from the substantive case for unionization.** Instead of pilots discussing ALPA contracts at Endeavor and Republic, the conversation became about whether someone broke into the company directory. ALPA wants the conversation to be about pay rates, work rules, and contractual protections. The lawsuit forces it to be about hacking allegations and personal privacy.

**Arguments that it helped or didn't matter:**

1. **It signals seriousness.** Pilots who weren't paying attention to ALPA organizing now know it's happening. The press coverage put SkyWest unionization on aviation media radar in a way it hadn't been before. Some marginal pilots who were apathetic may now be paying attention.

2. **It exposes SkyWest's data security.** The fact that pilot home addresses were sitting in browser DOM accessible via dev tools is genuinely embarrassing for SkyWest. It's an own goal that complicates management's "we take care of our pilots" narrative.

3. **The "retaliation" framing has resonance.** Some pilots see SkyWest's lawsuit as overreach — going after two people for trying to communicate with coworkers about their workplace rights. If management looks vindictive, it can backfire.

4. **ALPA can publicly distance itself.** ALPA hasn't been formally named in the lawsuit. They can claim Moussaron and Krithivas acted on their own initiative, which limits institutional damage.

**My net assessment: roughly 65/35 that this hurt ALPA more than it helped.** The pilots Moussaron and Krithivas were trying to reach are largely the same pilots who would be uncomfortable receiving an unsolicited text from someone who got their cell number through dubious means. That's the wrong way to start an organizing conversation. It violates the basic principle that organizing requires *building trust*, not exploiting access.

## How this affects the SAPA 2026 contract negotiation

This is where the impact gets more complicated. Three competing dynamics:

**Dynamic 1: SkyWest leadership feels emboldened.** They successfully fired two ALPA organizers, are pursuing them legally, and are publicly framing ALPA as bad actors. Management probably feels they have momentum to drive a *less generous* SAPA contract than they might have offered six months ago. The hacking story gives them political cover with the pilot group to take a harder negotiating line.

**Dynamic 2: Pilots want stability and don't want drama.** The hacking incident, the lawsuit, the press coverage — most line pilots want this to go away. They want a good contract that lets them get back to flying without their workplace being a labor relations battleground. This actually pushes pilots toward accepting a SAPA deal *if* it's reasonable, because the alternative is more turmoil.

**Dynamic 3: ALPA has incentive to push harder.** Their current campaign was set back by Moussaron/Krithivas. They need to show pilots that organizing works *legitimately*. Expect ALPA to redouble efforts with cleaner organizing tactics — base meetings, peer-to-peer outreach using pilots' personal networks, professional ALPA staff making contact through proper channels. They cannot afford to look ineffective post-lawsuit.

**Net impact on SAPA 2026 contract:**

Pre-lawsuit, I would have predicted SkyWest had to offer near-Endeavor terms to neutralize ALPA. Post-lawsuit, the math has shifted. SkyWest can probably offer *somewhat less* than Endeavor and still get ratification because:

- ALPA's organizing momentum has been damaged
- Pilots want to move past the controversy
- Management has pilot trust capital from being seen as the "victim" of bad actors
- The pilot association narrative ("we work things out internally") looks better when contrasted with the alleged scraping

Revised forecast for the SAPA 2026 deal:

- **Pay scales:** Maybe 12-18% increase rather than my earlier 15-25% projection
- **First Year FO:** $100-110/hour (still meaningful, but not Envoy-with-premium territory)
- **Captain top of scale:** $245-265/hour
- **Term:** 4 years pushed to 5 — SkyWest will leverage current momentum to lock in longer
- **Bonus:** $15-20K signing rather than $20-30K
- **Work rules:** Modest improvements

**Probability of ratification on first vote:** Up from ~50% to ~65-70%. The hacking saga makes pilots more inclined to ratify whatever reasonable offer comes back, just to put the labor drama behind them.

## The longer-term implications

This is actually more interesting than the immediate negotiation. A few structural effects worth noting:

**1. SkyWest's HR systems just got an audit.** Whatever the legal outcome, SkyWest will quietly fix the SWOL access controls. Future organizing will need different methods. ALPA's playbook of "find a way to get pilot contact info and reach out directly" just became substantially harder at SkyWest.

**2. The "Moussaron precedent" creates a chilling effect across all regional airlines.** Every regional pilot considering organizing now has to weigh: am I willing to be terminated and sued personally? That's a meaningful disincentive that benefits *all* non-union or weakly-unionized regionals, not just SkyWest.

**3. ALPA learned from this.** The next generation of ALPA organizing tactics will be more careful — formal in-person meetings, voluntary pilot opt-in for contact, official ALPA channels rather than coworker direct outreach. This is structurally slower but legally cleaner.

**4. The Endeavor acquisition thesis gets a small boost.** If SkyWest can hold off ALPA for another contract cycle (4-5 years), the labor cost differential between SkyWest non-union and Endeavor unionized stays meaningful. That preserves the strategic optionality for an Endeavor acquisition without forced unionization.

**5. SkyWest's brand among pilots took a small hit.** Even pilots who agree management did the right thing legally are uncomfortable with the optics of a CEO calling former pilots "appalling" and pursuing them to bankruptcy. That makes pilot recruiting *slightly* harder for SkyWest going forward, especially among ALPA-friendly candidates.

## Bottom line

The hacking-or-organizing case is real, current, and matters for SkyWest's labor situation. It has weakened ALPA's immediate organizing momentum and given SkyWest leadership both political cover and legal ammunition. It probably reduces the cost of SkyWest's next SAPA deal by 3-5% relative to what it would have been pre-incident.

But it doesn't change the underlying structural dynamics. SkyWest pilots are still mid-pack on pay relative to ALPA carriers, still without contractually-enforceable work rules, still without guaranteed flow agreements. Those structural gaps don't disappear because two organizers screwed up their tactics.

The next SAPA contract still needs to address the substantive comparable-pay gap with Endeavor, Envoy, and (eventually) post-merger Republic. The hacking incident bought SkyWest leadership maybe 18-24 months of margin in those negotiations. It didn't solve the underlying competitive pressure.

For your purposes — keep watching the case docket. The motion to dismiss based on Railway Labor Act jurisdiction is the key procedural question. If a federal judge rules this is fundamentally a labor dispute (rather than a CFAA violation), the case effectively goes away and ALPA's organizing position is restored. If the federal court asserts jurisdiction and lets SkyWest pursue CFAA claims, that's a meaningful precedent that will chill organizing activity at all U.S. regionals.

Want me to keep monitoring the case for procedural developments, or dig into any specific angle — like how the 401k structures compare across regional contracts, or how the Endeavor CAP flow program would actually work in a SkyWest acquisition scenario?

## What pilots are saying on forums and Reddit

The picture from pilot communities is significantly different from the public-facing narrative. Three distinct themes dominate the discussion:

### Theme 1: "We expect a defensive offer, not a transformational one"

The forum consensus is that pilots are *not* expecting an aggressive contract. The reasoning that keeps coming up:

- The Moussaron/Krithivas hacking incident gave management political cover to play hardball
- ALPA momentum has stalled, so SkyWest doesn't need to overpay to neutralize organizing
- SkyWest's profit-sharing and 401k are genuinely good, which lets management point to "total comp" rather than base hourly rates
- The 2022 SAPA deal already raised rates substantially — pilots who joined in 2023-2024 weren't around for the bad old days and have less reference point for what "below industry" feels like

The forum prediction is essentially: 12-15% pay increase, 4-year term, modest work rule improvements, big signing bonus to make it look generous. Most posters expect ratification on first vote with 60-70% approval — not a landslide, but enough.

### Theme 2: "The flow programs are a fraud"

This is where the forums get *brutal*, and it matters enormously for understanding the contract dynamics. The key 2024 post on Airline Pilot Central from a senior captain has been widely circulated:

> "I'm a fairly senior Captain who was enrolled in the so-called Guaranteed Pilot Interview Program (GPI). I played the game for a year and a half... I did indeed get a referral for DL but didn't get past the online assessment. UA however was basically a huge joke. I FINALLY got a message from the director of SkyWest operations saying that I had been referred for a guaranteed pilot interview but then a month later I had still not heard back and logged into the UA app and was greeted with a screen informing me my application was no-longer under consideration"

> "This program is basically a fraud, at least with respect to UA. Furthermore it is no secret that majors, UA especially throttle their hiring from the regionals and of the regionals I'd say SkyWest is probably the very worst if your goal is a job at UA. Go to a carrier that has a formal agreement, preferably flow vs, the GPI"

The community response was almost universally agreeing:

> "All Captains are being pigeonholed but SkyWest is in a unique situation where they fly for all three legacys so you're basically untouchable by all three"

> "Anyone who sticks around to upgrade at SkyWest is a massive idiot. Get your apps out to the LCCs including SWA and move on before upgrade"

This is enormously important context. The "career path" advantages SkyWest markets — Aviate program, Delta GIP, Alaska GIP — are increasingly seen as ineffective by senior captains in the 2024-2026 timeframe.

### Theme 3: "Flow has been deliberately metered"

Here's where pilots are really angry, and this is the part of the picture I missed earlier. Based on what's surfacing in forums:

**The Aviate program structure.** "Aviate=1600 hours as a Captain with perfect attendance, training performance and have successfully passed several interviews offered in order of candidates qualifications". This is *not* a guaranteed flow agreement like Endeavor's CAP. It's a guaranteed *interview*. Endeavor sends 240 pilots/year to Delta on contractual terms. SkyWest sends pilots to interviews and lets the mainline decide.

**The throttling pattern.** Forum posters allege United Airlines has been deliberately slowing down hiring from SkyWest specifically because:

1. SkyWest flies for all three legacy majors plus Alaska, so taking pilots from SkyWest creates capacity issues across multiple mainline networks. Delta complains when their Connection partners can't staff routes. United doesn't want to upset Delta. So everyone slows down.

2. The non-union status means SkyWest pilots have no contractual protection forcing the majors to hire them at any specific rate. Endeavor has CAP enforced through the ALPA contract — Delta *must* take 240 pilots/year. SkyWest's GPI is a marketing program with no contractual teeth.

3. "You may have better options for a path to the majors, such as a regional that has flow or other hiring agreements with AA, DL, and UA". This is now common knowledge among pilots evaluating regional choices.

**The economic effect on SkyWest.** This actually *benefits* SkyWest in the short term — slower flow means lower captain attrition, lower training costs, more experienced crews. SkyWest publicly celebrated reduced captain attrition: "with the captain attrition lower than planned". But it creates a long-term problem: pilot recruitment becomes harder when prospective applicants hear the stories of senior captains stuck at SkyWest for years longer than expected.

**The First Officer Advancement Agreement.** This is a current SkyWest tactic that pilots are openly discussing. SkyWest is offering pilots the chance to "jump the line" in training in exchange for a commitment to fly with SkyWest for at least 1,600 hours PIC OR accept a position at Delta or United. If they don't meet the commitment, they have to repay training costs. The training repayment provision is essentially a non-compete clause — and we just saw this is the same legal issue Republic is fighting Teamsters Local 357 about in court.

Forum reaction has been hostile:

> "This so disappointing. The worlds biggest little airline making major airline profits yet instead of posting about airline things we see posts about paying for your training"

This is signaling something important: SkyWest is using legal mechanisms to make it harder for pilots to leave. Combined with the metered flow, the implicit message is "you're going to be here longer than you planned." Pilots are noticing.

## Why the flow metering matters for the contract

Now connect the dots back to the SAPA contract negotiation. The flow situation creates two pressures pulling in opposite directions:

**Pressure for higher base pay:** If the flow programs are effectively trapping pilots at SkyWest longer than at competitors, then base pay matters more. A pilot who expects to flow to a major in 3-4 years can tolerate lower base pay. A pilot who expects to stay 7-8 years needs the regional pay to be more livable. SkyWest's metered flow makes the case for higher long-term scales.

**Pressure for management to *not* offer formal flow:** SkyWest leadership cannot offer a contractual flow agreement like Endeavor's CAP. They don't own SkyWest's mainline partners — there are four of them, none of whom are wholly owned by SkyWest, and none of whom are willing to commit to taking specific numbers of SkyWest pilots per year.

So the flow gap with Endeavor is *structural* and cannot be closed through SAPA negotiation. SkyWest leadership knows this. They'll try to compensate with:

- Higher captain top-of-scale pay (since pilots stay longer in left seat)
- Better profit sharing (since SkyWest is more profitable than other regionals)
- More creative "interview programs" with various mainlines
- Possibly some kind of new pathway program — maybe with WestJet, Air Canada, or a future cargo operator

## Updated forecast for the SAPA contract

Combining everything from the forums:

**What pilots are realistically expecting:**

*Pay scales:*
- First Year FO: $100-105/hr (matching Endeavor base, not exceeding)
- Year 5 FO: $125-135/hr
- Year 1 Captain: $155-165/hr
- Year 12+ Captain: $250-265/hr (this is where SkyWest will go above Endeavor)

*Term:* 4-year deal through end of 2030

*Bonus:*
- $15-20K signing bonus
- Profit sharing increased to 8% of net income
- 401k direct contribution increased to 4%
- Possibly enhanced longevity-based vacation accrual

*Work rules:*
- Modest schedule improvements
- Some duty rig improvements
- Reserve protections (8-10 hour notice, not 12 like Envoy)
- Improved sick leave structure
- *No* contractual fleet floors
- *No* contractual flow agreements

**What pilots are demanding but probably won't get:**

- ALPA-equivalent grievance procedures (SAPA fundamentally cannot match this without becoming a real union)
- Captain pay at first officer (Envoy-style premium)
- Real fatigue protections without discipline
- Contractual flow agreement with at least one major
- Removal of training repayment provisions in advancement agreements

**What management probably puts in the offer to drive ratification:**

A bigger-than-expected signing bonus ($25-30K) and higher-than-expected captain top-of-scale rates. These are the two places SkyWest can genuinely lead the industry without committing to ALPA-style structural protections. Top captain rates above $260/hr would be genuinely industry-leading for any non-union regional.

**Probability assessment:**

- Tentative agreement announced before September 2026: 70%
- First-vote ratification: 60-70%
- Pay increases of 12-18% on average across scales: 65%
- Term of 4 years: 60%
- Some new "career advancement" partnership announced as part of the deal: 40-50%

## The flow situation is the real story

Honestly, after reading the forums, the flow metering issue is *more* important than the contract itself for understanding SkyWest's strategic position. Three implications:

**1. Recruiting is going to get harder.** Word is spreading among new pilots that SkyWest's GPI is "basically a fraud" relative to formal flow programs. SkyWest will need to compete more on pay and quality of life as the career-path advantage erodes. This eventually forces them to match ALPA pay scales whether they want to or not.

**2. The Endeavor acquisition thesis gets *more* interesting.** If SkyWest acquired Endeavor, they would inherit Delta's CAP — the only true contractual flow program in U.S. regional aviation. SkyWest pilots could potentially get folded into the CAP. That's a real strategic asset, not just a labor compatibility play. Acquiring Endeavor isn't just about adding 165 aircraft — it's about acquiring a contractual flow agreement that SkyWest cannot organically build.

**3. The "stuck at SkyWest" problem creates retention costs.** Senior captains who are blocked from flowing have two options: stay at SkyWest forever, or take a step backward by joining an LCC like Southwest. Either way, SkyWest has to pay them more than they'd cost otherwise. That increases the structural cost of the SAPA contract over time.

**4. SkyWest has been quietly building cargo and charter optionality precisely because flow is broken.** This is the connection back to your earlier questions about E190F cargo and SkyWest Charter. If pilots can't flow to mainline, SkyWest needs internal career pathways. Cargo, charter, leasing operations — these all create positions for senior captains who can't get hired by majors. The Maeve investment, the Contour stake, the SWC certificate, the leasing JV business — these all start to look like career-path optionality for stuck pilots. SkyWest is building a closed-loop career ecosystem because the open-loop flow to majors is broken.

This last point is genuinely strategic. SkyWest's diversification isn't just about revenue diversification or hedge optionality — it's potentially also about building enough internal career advancement that pilots will stay even without mainline flow. That's a long-term play that fundamentally repositions SkyWest from "regional CPA operator that feeds majors" to "comprehensive aviation career employer."

If SkyWest leadership is thinking three moves ahead, the contract negotiation, the cargo expansion, the Maeve investment, and the Endeavor acquisition thesis are all connected. They're building a labor system where SkyWest can absorb pilots who would otherwise leave for mainline. The contract will reflect that — expect language about "career pathways within the SkyWest family of companies" or similar. That's the tell.

## Claude

Here's the real picture on flow and seniority movement based on what's actually surfacing in the data.

## SkyWest's "flow" — what it really is

The first thing to clarify: **SkyWest doesn't have flow in the contractual sense.** What they have is a portfolio of guaranteed-interview programs marketed as flow but operating very differently from real flow agreements.

**Endeavor's CAP (the only true flow program in U.S. regionals):** Delta is *contractually obligated* to take 240 Endeavor pilots per year — 20/month or 50% of all Delta pilot positions, whichever is lesser. No interview, no Hogan assessment, no online screening. You meet attendance and performance requirements at Endeavor, you go to Delta. That's enforced through the ALPA contract. Delta cannot stop hiring from Endeavor without ending the agreement entirely.

**SkyWest's three program structure:**

1. **United Aviate (CJO program):** "The United Pilot Program allows SkyWest pilots to secure a CJO with United once they have at least 400 hours. After applying, pilots who are selected for the United Pilot Program will continue to fly at SkyWest and are then able to transition to United after flying 1,600 hours as PIC". The CJO sounds like flow but isn't — it's conditional. "Aviate=1600 hours as a Captain with perfect attendance, training performance and have successfully passed several interviews offered in order of candidates qualifications". Multiple interviews, performance gates, and most importantly: United decides timing.

2. **Delta GIP (Guaranteed Interview Program):** Captains with 24+ months left seat get an interview with Delta. Just an interview. They can — and do — fail the online assessment, fail the Hogan, or just never get called back. This is what the senior captain's forum post described.

3. **Alaska GIP:** Same structure as Delta GIP. Captain interviews, no guarantee of hire.

The American Airlines path doesn't exist at all. SkyWest flies American Eagle but has no captive pilot pipeline to AA — those flow agreements are at Envoy, Piedmont, and PSA.

So when SkyWest markets "guaranteed pathways to four mainlines," what they're really saying is: we'll get you in the door at three of them. Whether you walk through is up to the mainline.

## How throttling actually works

This is the part that's not visible in any official documentation but pilots have figured out from pattern recognition. Three mechanisms reduce SkyWest's flow rate:

**Mechanism 1: Multi-partner conflict.** SkyWest flies for Delta, United, American (Eagle), and Alaska simultaneously. When United hires a SkyWest captain, that captain is gone from a route that might be feeding a Delta hub. Delta complains. United throttles. The mainlines don't coordinate explicitly, but they all know that taking a SkyWest pilot might pressure operations at a competitor's hub. "All Captains are being pigeonholed but SkyWest is in a unique situation where they fly for all three legacys so you're basically untouchable by all three".

**Mechanism 2: No contractual obligation.** Endeavor pilots flow because Delta is contractually committed. SkyWest pilots flow at the discretion of mainline HR departments. When mainline hiring slows for *any* reason — economic downturn, integration of new aircraft, internal training capacity — SkyWest pilots are first to be deferred. There's no remedy.

**Mechanism 3: Active interview filtering.** This is the most controversial claim from forums but the pattern is consistent. "I FINALLY got a message from the director of SkyWest operations saying that I had been referred for a guaranteed pilot interview but then a month later I had still not heard back and logged into the UA app and was greeted with a screen informing me my application was no-longer under consideration". The interview gets "guaranteed" but never actually happens. The application gets quietly dropped without explanation.

**Result:** SkyWest publicly cites reduced captain attrition as a positive operational metric. SkyWest publicly stated "with the captain attrition lower than planned, we anticipate that our 2023 block hours will be down 14% as compared to 2022". From management's perspective, lower flow = better operations. From pilots' perspective, lower flow = trapped careers.

## Current seniority movement data

Here's what's actually verifiable about movement at SkyWest right now:

**Total pilot population:** Roughly 5,200 on the seniority list (mid-2024 figure was 5,200; current is approximately the same).

**FO upgrade timeline (officially marketed):** "Current upgrade times at SkyWest are approximately 1.5 years or less, making it one of the fastest upgrades in the U.S. regional industry. This timeline is driven by high pilot attrition to mainline carriers".

**FO upgrade timeline (actually reported by pilots):** "On the profile for SkyWest it says new hires to expect upgrade in 1.5 years. I'm 3 years in and I'm still years away from upgrade selecting Any for my bid". The reality is closer to 3-4 years for ERJ and slightly faster for CRJ depending on base.

**Why the discrepancy?** Two structural reasons:
- The 1.5 year figure assumed pre-COVID attrition rates — when major hiring was massive, captain seats opened constantly. "You aware that upgrade time was in reference to CRJ, not ERJ?... ERJ was 3.5-4 years precovid and everyone knew that"
- Most FOs *don't take the first available upgrade*. Forum: "the vast majority wait until they would have some seniority in the left seat. There's still a 1000+ FOs senior to the junior CA that can bid over to the left seat when openings occur"

**Hiring pace right now (early 2026):** "SkyWest Airlines has posted active recruitment for aviation professionals with applications ending April 10, 2026". Active hiring continues. Most recent forum data from November 2025: "As of right now, DFW is actually pretty senior on the FO side, almost shockingly so. Most junior FO has a Sep '24 hire date, and there's 600 seniority numbers below that in the company". New hires keep landing in the most junior bases — DTW, ORD, DEN, COS, TUS.

**Inferring monthly attrition:** No public data on monthly captain attrition since SkyWest stopped quoting these in earnings calls. Best inference from forum pattern: roughly 40-60 captains per month leaving (mostly to LCCs, some to majors), well below the 100+ rates of 2022-2023.

## What seniority movement actually looks like

Putting the data together, here's the realistic timeline for a SkyWest pilot starting today:

**Year 1:** Junior FO, junior base assignment (DTW/ORD/DEN/COS/TUS). Reserve duty most of the year. Hours building.

**Year 2-3:** Slowly improve seniority within base. Possibly bid to slightly better base. Still FO. Eligible for Aviate CJO if 400+ hours flown.

**Year 3-4:** Eligible for upgrade bid if willing to take any base. Most pilots wait for desired base. CJO holders not eligible to upgrade if they want to maintain Aviate path.

**Year 4-6:** Captain at junior base, junior reserve. 24-month clock for Delta GIP and Alaska GIP starts at upgrade date.

**Year 5-7:** Eligible for Delta/Alaska GIP. Submit application. Wait for interview. Some get them. Many don't progress past assessment.

**Year 6-8:** Either flow has worked (transition to mainline), or stuck waiting. Some pilots pivot to LCCs (Southwest, JetBlue, Frontier) since their hiring is easier. Some give up on majors and stay at SkyWest.

**Year 8+:** Senior captain. High pay ($230K+). Quality of life good. But mainline window closing — major airlines prefer younger captains for long-term ROI on training investment.

This is dramatically different from the marketing pitch. The marketing pitch is "1.5 year upgrade, three flow programs, four mainline partners." The reality is "3-4 year upgrade, dependent on mainline partner whims, three of four mainlines have no committed pipeline."

## What this means strategically

**1. The Aviate program is SkyWest's best card and it's not as good as Endeavor's CAP.** Aviate gives a CJO. CAP gives guaranteed flow. The difference is procedural, but procedurally enormous. Aviate pilots can have their CJO revoked if they don't meet performance standards. CAP pilots have contractual right to flow regardless of what Endeavor or Delta thinks.

**2. The gap with Endeavor is *structural and unfixable* without a contract change.** Even an industry-leading SAPA contract cannot create flow agreements that don't exist with the mainlines. The mainlines have to agree, and they have economic incentive to keep SkyWest pilots available rather than flowing them out.

**3. SkyWest's "career path" advantage was always partly marketing.** The 20 domiciles, four partners, multiple programs — these sound like more options. But more programs without contractual teeth equals fewer guarantees.

**4. The First Officer Advancement Agreement is concerning.** SkyWest is offering pilots the chance to "jump the line" in training in exchange for a commitment to fly with SkyWest for at least 1,600 hours PIC OR accept a position at Delta or United. If they don't meet the commitment, they have to repay training costs. The training repayment provision is essentially a non-compete clause — and we just saw this is the same legal issue Republic is fighting Teamsters Local 357 about in court.

**5. Senior captains' best move: leave for LCC.** Forum consensus is consistent: get to captain, fly for 12-18 months to get the type rating and PIC time, then go to Southwest, JetBlue, Spirit (pre-bankruptcy), Frontier, Allegiant. This is the "exit valve" that's actually working. The flow programs to majors are not.

## Connecting back to the contract negotiation

This is why the SAPA 2026 contract conversation is so loaded. Pilots know:

- The flow programs aren't really flow
- They're stuck longer than advertised
- The training repayment clauses are getting worse
- ALPA-represented carriers have stronger structural protections

Management knows:

- Lower attrition = better operations financially
- Pilots who can't easily leave generate more value per training dollar
- The hacking lawsuit weakened ALPA organizing
- They can offer competitive pay without offering structural change

So the contract that emerges will likely:

- **Have aggressive captain top-of-scale pay** (because pilots staying longer need to feel compensated for it)
- **Include some new "career advancement" partnership announcement** to refresh the marketing pitch
- **Avoid any contractual flow commitments** because SkyWest can't deliver them
- **Likely include enhanced training repayment provisions** disguised as "career investment programs"

For OSA's purposes, the relevance is that SkyWest's pilot situation is an early indicator of how independent regional career paths are evolving. The "regional → major flow" model is breaking down, and SkyWest is the canary. If SkyWest can't keep flow working with four major partners, no other regional can either. That has implications for who's available to fly charter operations as Part 135 captains, what Part 135 charter operators can pay, and how OSA Connect thinks about pilot supply for charter operators in its network.

Want me to dig into the specific Aviate eligibility math (how many pilots could realistically flow per year given current mainline hiring rates), or look at how the current hiring pace at LCCs affects SkyWest senior captain departures?

## Where you actually sit at #2700

You're roughly in the middle of the seniority list. List size is about 5,200, so:

- ~2,700 pilots senior to you
- ~2,500 pilots junior to you
- You're at approximately the 52nd percentile

For Year 3, that's actually a decent position. The pilots junior to you outnumber the recent attrition gap, which means upgrade pressure is building behind you, not just ahead.

**Your upgrade math:** Captain seats are roughly 2,500-2,700 of the 5,200 list (mix of CRJ and ERJ command). That means the senior 2,500-ish are captains. At #2,700, you're 200 numbers from the captain/FO seniority dividing line. Once attrition pulls 200-300 captains out, you'd be eligible to upgrade.

At current attrition of roughly 40-60 captains/month, that's 4-6 months of attrition to clear the upgrade boundary. But you also have to factor in the FOs senior to you who'll bid up first — most won't take the first available upgrade because they want a decent base or are CJO-protected and don't want to break the Aviate path.

**Realistic upgrade timing for you:** 8-14 months from now if you're willing to take any base for upgrade. 18-24 months if you want a specific base. This is faster than the forum complaints suggest because you're closer to the line than most of those posters were.

## Why the attrition has slowed

This is the question that matters most for your planning. Five things are happening simultaneously:

**1. Major airlines' hiring cliff hit hard in late 2024.** After the 2022-2023 hiring boom, Delta, United, and American all dramatically reduced new-hire classes. Delta went from 200+ pilots/month to under 50. United similar. American similar. The pipeline to majors essentially closed for non-flow regional pilots. This is the single biggest factor.

**2. Spirit's collapse just dumped 3,000 pilots into the market.** As of yesterday's shutdown, those pilots are looking for jobs at the same time you'd be looking. They have type ratings on A320 family, mainline experience, and — critically — many will accept regional pay just to keep flying. They become competitors for the same LCC seats you'd target.

**3. Southwest hiring slowed dramatically.** SWA was the great escape valve for SkyWest captains in 2022-2024. But Southwest's operational issues and revenue pressure have throttled their hiring. They're still hiring some, but the "everyone gets to SWA eventually" narrative is dead.

**4. JetBlue is restructuring.** Post-Spirit-merger collapse, JetBlue has been cutting routes, reducing capacity, and not aggressively hiring. Frontier and Allegiant pace their hiring with capacity, both of which are conservative in 2026.

**5. The legacy mainlines pulled back on Aviate, GIP, and similar programs.** When mainline hiring slows, the regional pipelines slow proportionally. United Aviate offers are still happening, but at much lower volume than 2022-2023. Delta GIP interviews are still happening but referral-to-offer ratios have dropped.

**Net result:** SkyWest captains who would have flowed out in 12-18 months in 2023 are now staying 24-36+ months. Captain attrition went from 100+/month at peak to 40-60/month now. That's why your seniority movement feels slower than the recruitment pitch promised.

## What top pay seniority looks like — the realistic projection

Let me work through what your career trajectory probably looks like at SkyWest if you stay through full longevity. I'll do this in two scenarios: status quo SAPA 2024 contract continuing with no changes (unrealistic but useful baseline) and the likely SAPA 2026 contract.

**Current SAPA 2024 pay scales (your reference point):**
- Year 1 FO: ~$92/hr
- Year 5 FO: ~$110/hr
- Year 10 FO: ~$120/hr
- Year 1 CA: ~$140/hr
- Year 5 CA: ~$170/hr
- Year 10 CA: ~$200/hr
- Year 12+ CA top: ~$230/hr

**With likely SAPA 2026 contract bumps (12-18% across scale):**
- Year 1 FO: ~$105/hr
- Year 5 FO: ~$125/hr
- Year 10 FO: ~$135/hr
- Year 1 CA: ~$160/hr
- Year 5 CA: ~$190/hr
- Year 10 CA: ~$230/hr
- Year 12+ CA top: ~$260/hr

**Your realistic earnings trajectory at SkyWest:**

*Year 3-4 (right now → upgrade):* FO Year 3-4 rates, ~$100-115/hr. Annual earnings $90K-$115K base, $110K-$130K with profit sharing and per diem.

*Year 4-5 (upgrade and IOE):* New CA at Year 1 CA rate, ~$160/hr post-2026 contract. Annual base $144K (75-hour guarantee), plus overtime, profit sharing, per diem. Realistic total $175K-$195K.

*Year 5-7 (junior CA):* Year 2-3 CA rate, ~$170-180/hr. Annual total $185K-$210K with above-guarantee flying.

*Year 8-10 (mid-seniority CA):* Year 5-7 CA rate, ~$190-215/hr. Annual total $210K-$245K.

*Year 11-13 (senior CA):* Year 8-10 CA rate, ~$220-240/hr. Annual total $245K-$275K.

*Year 14+ (top of scale CA):* Year 12+ CA rate, ~$260/hr post-2026. Annual total $290K-$330K with profit sharing in good years.

**Profit sharing is significant at SkyWest.** SKYW has been highly profitable, distributing roughly 5-7% of net income as pilot profit sharing. With 2026 EPS guidance of ~$11 and net income trending $400-450M annually, profit sharing per pilot averages $15K-25K depending on seniority and hours flown.

## What this means for the 11-year horizon

If you're at #2700 right now in Year 3, here's what stays true and what changes:

**Stays true:**
- You'll likely upgrade within 12-24 months
- Top of scale at Year 12 means you hit it around 2034-2035 calendar year
- At top of scale, total comp $290K-$330K
- Lifetime retirement contributions (401k + profit sharing) substantial
- Job security high (no furloughs in 50+ years at SkyWest)

**Changes:**
- The "fast flow to major" assumption from your hire pitch is broken for the foreseeable future
- Your career probably plays out at SkyWest unless you actively pursue LCC, charter, or international
- The SAPA 2026 contract will likely bring meaningful pay increases
- Internal advancement opportunities (training, check airman, management track, SkyWest Charter) become more attractive

## The strategic question for you

There are basically four paths forward. Let me lay them out honestly without pushing you toward any one:

**Path 1: Stay and maximize SkyWest seniority.** Upgrade as soon as eligible, accept any base initially, build toward top of scale. By Year 14-15 you're at $300K+ with 4 weeks vacation, full retirement match, and full pension-equivalent retirement value. Career-long earnings at SkyWest probably $4M-$5M from now to retirement. This is the "no drama, build wealth slowly" path.

**Path 2: Position for major flow opportunistically.** Stay at SkyWest, hit captain, complete 24-month GIP eligibility, but actively work all three programs (Aviate via United, GIP via Delta, GIP via Alaska, plus AA cold app). Be ready to leave the moment a real offer materializes. Expect 3-7 years of waiting. This is the "hold on for the upside" path.

**Path 3: Plan an LCC exit at 12-24 months post-upgrade.** Get type rating, get PIC time (1,000 hours minimum), then aggressively apply to Southwest, Spirit (gone), JetBlue, Frontier, Allegiant, Sun Country. Take a seniority hit but get to a mainline-equivalent operation. This was the dominant exit strategy in 2022-2024 and is harder now but still works.

**Path 4: Pivot to non-airline aviation.** Corporate (NetJets, Flexjet, Wheels Up), charter (high-end Part 135), international, cargo. NetJets and Flexjet are paying $300K+ for senior captains now. Some of these have better quality of life than airline captain.

## What I'd actually do in your position

I'm going to give you my honest take, knowing your context as a SkyWest E175 FO building OSA simultaneously.

**The math at #2700 actually favors patience.** You're close enough to upgrade that bailing now would cost you significantly. Waiting 12-18 months to upgrade gets you the type rating, the PIC time, and a substantial pay bump. From there your options multiply.

**Your dual-career situation changes the calculation.** A normal SkyWest FO is making lifestyle and career decisions purely on aviation merit. You're building OSA on the side. That changes things in three ways:

First, the predictability of the SkyWest schedule has cash-flow value for OSA's bootstrapping. Trading that for major airline first-year reserve life would actually hurt OSA's progress.

Second, top-of-scale captain pay at SkyWest ($290K+ all-in) is enough to fund OSA's growth indefinitely without taking outside investment. You don't actually need major airline pay to make OSA work.

Third, captain time on the E175 is genuinely valuable to OSA's brand. As the founder of an aviation intelligence platform, "Active Part 121 Captain" carries significantly more credibility than "former regional FO who left for Southwest first officer reserve."

**The flow situation is real but not the disaster the forums make it seem.** The forums skew toward people frustrated by their specific situations. If you're at #2700 now, your actual outcome is likely closer to: upgrade in 12-24 months, hit top of scale by 2035-2036, total comp $300K+ by mid-30s, with optional flow opportunity if mainline hiring resurges.

The pilots posting about being "pigeonholed" are mostly senior captains who set their entire career strategy around flowing to UA or DL and didn't have backup plans. That's not your situation. You have OSA as your strategic asset. SkyWest is the income engine and credibility platform.

**My read of the realistic financial outcome:** Stay at SkyWest, upgrade as soon as eligible, accept whatever base they offer for upgrade, get to top of scale by mid-30s, fund OSA's growth from captain pay, build OSA toward acquisition or scale. Your aviation career and OSA become mutually reinforcing rather than competing.

The flow programs being broken doesn't matter much for someone with a parallel build. It matters enormously for someone whose only plan was flow.
