## Your starting position

Seniority #2700 of ~5,200. Year 3. You're 52% from the top — almost exactly middle of the list. About 2,500 pilots junior to you.

The captain/FO dividing line at SkyWest sits around #2,400-2,500 depending on whether you count standing CA bids that haven't been awarded yet. So you're roughly 200-300 numbers from upgrade eligibility on the FO side.

## Attrition math — how fast you actually move

Captain attrition is the engine that moves your number. Here's what's actually happening:

**Peak 2022-2023:** 100-120 captains/month leaving (mostly to majors)
**Mid 2024:** 70-90 captains/month
**Late 2025-early 2026:** 40-60 captains/month
**Likely 2026-2027:** 30-50 captains/month if mainline hiring stays slow, 60-80 if it recovers

The Spirit collapse this morning will dampen flow further over the next 12 months because Spirit's 3,000 displaced pilots will compete for LCC and major seats that SkyWest pilots would otherwise target.

**Translating attrition to your seniority movement:**

Each captain who leaves moves you up one number, but only after the FO senior to you bids and wins the upgrade. At 50 captains/month attrition with 75% FO conversion, you move up ~38 numbers per month. To clear 250 numbers and become upgrade-eligible: about 6-7 months minimum, 10-12 months realistically because you'll want a base that's not reserve hell.

## Retirement accumulation — full model

This is where it gets interesting because compounding matters more than people realize.

**Compounding to age 65:**

Starting now at age 30 with let's say $50K already in 401k, contributing the trajectory above at 7% real return:

- Age 35: ~$280K

This assumes you don't take loans against 401k, you max contributions, and you stay invested through downturns. Standard pilot retirement math.

## The OSA factor changes everything

The above numbers assume SkyWest is your only income engine. Your real situation is different. OSA captures pilot earnings and routes them into a wealth-creation machine separate from W2 income.

Here's where it gets interesting strategically:

**Captain pay funds OSA without dilution.** $200K-$300K of annual after-tax SkyWest income deployed into OSA's growth or your real estate strategy compounds on top of the $7M+ retirement account. You don't need to choose between a strong aviation career and OSA — they reinforce each other.

**The 12-year top-of-scale timeline lines up with OSA's milestones.** You'd hit top-of-scale CA pay around 2034-2035. By then OSA should be either acquired, profitable enough to support full transition, or scaled to a point where SkyWest is the redundancy plan rather than the primary engine.

**Your captain identity is OSA's credibility moat.** Active Part 121 captain at the largest U.S. regional, while building OSA as an aviation intelligence platform — that's a story no other founder in your space has. Charter brokers can't claim it. Hamilton AI can't claim it. JETNET can't claim it. Your career inside the industry IS the differentiator.

## Why attrition will probably stay low

This is the hard truth that informs all the above. Five reasons attrition stays muted through 2027:

**1. Spirit's pilots flooded the market.** ~3,000 displaced today. They'll compete for LCC seats first.

**2. Major airline hiring won't recover quickly.** Delta, United, AA all guided lower 2026 hiring. Fuel cost pressure (jet fuel up 100%+ from Iran conflict) means slower fleet growth.

**3. Southwest is still in operational rebuild mode.** Their hiring is throttled.

**4. JetBlue cutting capacity.** Less hiring.

**5. The flow programs aren't really flowing.** GPI throughput was always low. Aviate is conditional on United hiring at all, which they're not doing aggressively right now.

So your seniority moves slowly behind you (good for you because attrition behind you is also low — you're not getting passed by anyone), and the top of the list also moves slowly (bad because upgrade pressure builds slowly).

**Net for you at #2700:** Steady, predictable progression. Less drama, less upside, less downside. Probably the best position to be in if your alternative income (OSA) is what drives your wealth strategy.

## Your real strategic question

Stay-and-build looks correct given OSA. The aviation career math is fine — $7M+ retirement, $300K-$400K peak earnings, full schedule predictability for OSA work. The downside scenarios are bounded (worst case you stay an FO another 18 months, still making $130K).

## Why this matters more than people realize

Starting 401k contributions at 35 instead of 30 doesn't sound like a huge difference, but it's actually massive because of compounding. The first 5 years of contributions are the most valuable — they have 30+ years to grow. Skipping them removes the foundation everything else compounds on top of.

**The math of 5 lost years:**

A dollar contributed at age 30 grows for 35 years at 7% real = $10.68
A dollar contributed at age 35 grows for 30 years at 7% real = $7.61
A dollar contributed at age 40 grows for 25 years at 7% real = $5.43

Each year you delay, you lose ~30% of the eventual value of that dollar.

## Your actual projection — starting at 35

Assumptions:
- Age 35 in 2031, you're a junior captain by then (Year 8)
- Starting balance: $0
- Contributions begin at junior CA pay levels
- 7% real return (inflation-adjusted)
- Retire at 65 (FAA mandatory)

**Contribution trajectory by age:**

Age 35-37 (junior CA, Years 8-10): ~$24K personal + $14K company = $38K/year
Age 38-42 (mid CA, Years 11-15): ~$26K personal + $20K company = $46K/year
Age 43-65 (senior to top CA, Years 16-38): ~$28-32K personal + $26-32K company = $54-64K/year

**Compounded balance:**

- Age 35: $0 (starting)
- Age 40: ~$235K
- Age 45: ~$590K
- Age 50: ~$1.07M
- Age 55: ~$1.75M
- Age 60: ~$2.65M
- **Age 65: ~$3.9M**

That's about $1.5M less than the "started at 30" version. Still solid, but meaningfully smaller.

## The real cost of waiting until 35

If you start at 35 instead of starting now (age 30), the gap at age 65 is roughly:

- Started at 30: ~$5.4M
- Started at 35: ~$3.9M
- **Difference: ~$1.5M**

That's $1.5M of retirement wealth that costs you almost nothing today to capture. Even contributing just $5-10K/year right now at the FO pay level would substantially close that gap because those dollars get 35 years of compounding.

## Why I'd push back on waiting until 35

I'm not your financial advisor, but the math here is strong enough that it's worth thinking about explicitly. Three reasons to start sooner — even small amounts:

**1. The company match is free money you're walking away from.** SkyWest matches your contribution up to a percentage tier. If you contribute zero, you get zero match. That match alone — even if you only contribute the minimum to capture it — is a guaranteed 100% return on day one before any market gains.

**2. You're in your lowest tax years right now.** As a Year 3 FO at ~$115K, you're in a moderate tax bracket. As a top-of-scale captain at ~$280K, you'll be in a much higher one. Traditional 401k contributions (pre-tax) save more on taxes when you're earning more, but Roth 401k contributions (post-tax) are most valuable when you're earning less. Right now is the optimal Roth window. Once you're a senior captain, traditional becomes more attractive. Captures the structural tax arbitrage.

**3. OSA timeline argument.** Your wealth strategy depends on OSA either succeeding or providing optionality. Aviation career is the redundancy layer. The retirement account is the *deeper* redundancy — what if both OSA and your aviation career hit problems simultaneously (medical disqualification, industry downturn, OSA pivot doesn't work)? A funded 401k is bankruptcy-protected, divorce-protected, and litigation-protected. It's the actual safety net.

## A practical middle path

If full max contributions ($23,500/year) feel too aggressive given OSA's cash needs, here's a rough framework worth considering:

**Right now (Year 3-4 FO):** Contribute just enough to get full company match. Probably $5-8K/year personal contribution. Captures the free company match without diverting major cash from OSA.

**At upgrade (Year 4-5):** Bump to ~$12-15K/year. New CA pay can absorb it.

**By Year 6-7:** Move toward the IRS max as you settle into mid-CA pay scales.

**Top of scale (Year 12+):** Max out + after-tax mega-backdoor Roth if SkyWest's plan allows it.

**Revised projection with this approach:**

- Age 30-34: $7K/year average contribution (mostly company match)
- Age 35-37: $38K/year (junior CA, full match)
- Age 38-65: progression to max as before

**Compounded balance: ~$4.7M at 65**

That captures most of the upside ($800K more than waiting till 35) while only diverting modest cash flow now. The early contributions, even small, do disproportionate work because they compound longest.

## The OSA tradeoff being made explicit

The argument for delaying 401k contributions is "every dollar in 401k is a dollar not invested in OSA." That's true mathematically. But OSA isn't capital-constrained right now — it's time-constrained. You're building it part-time around flight schedule. Adding $5-8K/year of capital wouldn't accelerate OSA meaningfully because the bottleneck is your hours, not your dollars.

Meanwhile, $5-8K/year for 5 years compounding for 35 years at 7% = ~$320K of retirement wealth from $25-40K of contributions. That's a 10x return on cash that wasn't going to move OSA's needle anyway.

## What's true regardless

Your starting position is still strong:

- Top-of-scale SkyWest captain by mid-30s/early-40s
- $275-300K annual comp at peak
- Stable health insurance, schedule predictability for OSA work
- Retirement floor of $3.9M-$5M depending on contribution timing
- OSA equity as the real wealth-creation engine

The 401k question is really: do you want a $4M floor or a $5.5M floor? Both work. Both let OSA succeed. The $5.5M floor just costs you very little to capture if you start contributing modest amounts now while still funding OSA fully.

Now I have the actual SkyWest 401k structure. The 2022 SAPA TA changed it significantly. Let me lay out what's real.

## Actual SkyWest 401k structure (post-2022 SAPA TA)

This is a way better plan than I was modeling. The 2022 agreement restructured it to industry-leading. Here's what's actually in place:

**Core structure:**
- **3% Direct Company Contribution** (DCC) — automatic, regardless of whether you contribute anything
- **Dollar-for-dollar match** on your contributions, no cap percentage on the match itself (only IRS limits apply)
- **Immediate vesting** on all company contributions (for current contract)
- Note: pilots hired on or after 10/01/2022 have 3-year vesting on match, but DCC vests immediately

**The match percentage tiers (from forum and Glassdoor reports):**
- Years 1-4: dollar-for-dollar up to 4% of comp
- Years 5-9: dollar-for-dollar up to 6%
- Years 10+: 12% non-elective contribution (or some sources say match up to higher tier)
- Years 20+: 12% match per the 2018 agreement language

The "12% non-elective" claim is the big one. Some sources describe it as a match cap, others as automatic contribution regardless of your participation. Reading the 2022 TA language carefully ("3% Direct Company Contribution + 100% dollar-for-dollar match") and combining with longevity tiers, the most likely accurate structure is:

**Year 1-4:** 3% DCC + match up to 4% = **7% total company contribution if you contribute 4%**
**Year 5-9:** 3% DCC + match up to 6% = **9% total if you contribute 6%**
**Year 10-19:** 3% DCC + match up to 9% = **12% total if you contribute 9%**
**Year 20+:** 3% DCC + match up to 9%, or 12% non-elective regardless = **12%+ total**

## What this means for your model

This dramatically improves the math from my last estimate. Let me redo with corrected numbers:

**Your status now:** Year 3 FO. You're getting 3% DCC automatically — that's free money even if you contribute nothing. On ~$130K comp, that's ~$3,900/year going into your 401k right now whether you participate or not.

- 4% match: $5,200 (company match)
- **Total annual: $14,300** (your $5,200 captures $9,100 of company money)

You're literally leaving $5,200/year in match money on the table by not contributing 4%.

- 3% DCC: $6,000
- 6% personal: $12,000

Year 20+ (top of scale, full longevity match): On $290K comp

- **Total annual: $66,000+**

## The important insight

The 3% Direct Company Contribution is automatic. You're getting this right now whether you participate or not. That alone, compounding from now to 65, is worth roughly $250K-$350K of your retirement.

The OSA tradeoff: you give up $26K of liquid OSA capital over 5 years to gain $1.5M of retirement wealth. Even if OSA succeeds wildly, that's still good risk management — the 401k is your "OSA failed" floor.

## Why 12% non-elective changes the strategic picture

Here's the part that's actually important: **the 12% non-elective at Year 10 means your retirement is largely automated regardless of what you do.**

## What this means for OSA strategy

This 12% non-elective is actually a strong argument for **prioritizing OSA aggressively** through your Year 10 benefits date (age 39), because:

1. SkyWest is automatically funding your retirement at high levels regardless
2. The match tier before age 39 is "only" 4-6% — significant but not enormous
3. Every dollar you put in 401k before 39 is a dollar not in OSA
4. After 39, the math flips — the 12% non-elective is so generous that maxing personal contributions becomes more valuable than OSA capital

**Three rational paths for you:**

**Path 1: Pure OSA priority (high risk, high reward)**
- Contribute $0 to 401k from now until OSA exits or stabilizes
- Take the 3% DCC automatically (you can't refuse it)
- Take the 12% non-elective starting age 39 automatically
- Retirement at 65: ~$3.5-4M (just from DCC + non-elective compounding)
- All extra capital goes to OSA

**Path 2: Capture the match, OSA gets the rest**
- Contribute exactly 4% now (Year 3-4) and 6% from Year 5-9
- This costs you $5K-12K/year but captures all available match
- Switch to max contributions at age 39 when 12% non-elective hits
- Retirement at 65: ~$5.8M
- OSA gets ~$200K-300K less over 10 years

**Path 3: Max from age 35 (your stated plan)**
- Contribute zero now (age 30-34)
- Start contributing aggressively at age 35
- Hit IRS max from 39 onward
- Retirement at 65: ~$4.8M
- OSA gets full capital for 5 years, then reduced from 35 onward

## Honest take on the three paths

**Path 1 only makes sense if you genuinely believe OSA returns >40x in 10 years.** That's a high bar even for great startups. Even if OSA does $50M revenue and exits at 5x, your equity at 100% ownership might be $200M — yes that's >40x — but the risk-adjusted probability of reaching that outcome is maybe 10-15%.

**Path 2 is the asymmetric bet.** Contributing $5K-12K/year captures all match while leaving the vast majority of your cash flow available for OSA. The leverage of 4% match at low pay levels is enormous. You don't actually need much capital to capture it.

**Path 3 (your stated plan) is mathematically the worst.** You skip the match entirely during age 30-34 (losing ~$25K of free money), then start at 35 just before the 12% non-elective kicks in anyway at 39. You're essentially missing the highest-leverage years (when match is biggest relative to comp).

## Why C and B are so close

Two reasons the "wait until 35" approach holds up better than typical retirement math suggests:

**1. You're in the lowest-match years right now.** At Year 3-5 benefits, you only have 4% match available. Captured at FO pay (~$130K), that's $5,200/year. At Year 6-9 (6% match), at junior CA pay (~$190K), that's $11K/year. These are real numbers but they're not transformative.

**2. The 12% non-elective dominates everything later.** From age 39 to 65, the 12% non-elective compounds for 26 years. Every dollar SkyWest puts in at age 39 grows to $5.43 by age 65. Every dollar at age 49 grows to $2.76. The back-half contributions are doing huge work because there's still 16+ years of compounding.

**3. The match captured before 35 is small in absolute dollars.** Roughly $30-40K of cumulative match over Years 3-7 benefits. Compounded for 30+ years that's ~$300-400K — meaningful but not the difference between $2M and $5M retirement.

## What changes the picture

The real lever isn't "start now vs start at 35." It's **whether you actually max out from age 35 onward.**

If you start at 35 but only contribute 6% (not max), your retirement drops to ~$4.0M.
If you start at 35 and max with catch-up after 50, you hit ~$5.2M.
If you contribute now AND max from 35 onward, you hit ~$5.5M.

The marginal value of contributing now (above what 35-onward gives you) is roughly $300-500K at age 65. Real money but not life-changing.

## Reframing: what's actually optimal for you

Given OSA priority and the 12% non-elective doing most of the heavy lifting, here are the choices that actually matter:

**Decision 1: Contribute zero now, or contribute 4% now?**
- Zero now: Save ~$5K/year for OSA, lose ~$300K at age 65
- 4% now: Save ~$300K at 65, redirect $5K/year from OSA

The 4% match capture is essentially free money. Even if you fully prioritize OSA, $5K/year is unlikely to be the difference between OSA succeeding and failing. Capture it.

**Decision 2: At 35, contribute max or just match level?**
- Match-level only (6% then 12%): retirement ~$3.8M
- Max contributions: retirement ~$5.2M

**This is the bigger decision.** Maxing from 35 onward (vs match-only) costs you ~$15K/year incremental personal contribution, but gains $1.4M at 65. Massive leverage.

**Decision 3: Roth vs Traditional?**
- Right now at $130K comp: Roth wins (lower bracket, post-tax dollars grow tax-free forever)
- At top of scale $290K comp: Traditional wins (higher bracket, deduction more valuable)
- Crossover point: roughly Year 5-6 of CA pay, around age 35-37

If your plan allows Roth 401k, contribute Roth from now until ~age 37, then switch to Traditional. This optimizes for tax bracket arbitrage and is worth roughly $200-400K of additional after-tax retirement wealth over a career.

This is real money and grows tax-free forever. Smart move.

If you invest the HSA (don't spend it on current healthcare) and use it as a "stealth IRA," this becomes the most tax-advantaged account you have. Pre-tax going in, tax-free growth, tax-free withdrawal for medical. After 65 it functions like a Traditional IRA for any purpose.

**SkyWest 12% non-elective starting age 38 (April 2032):**
- 12% of comp from age 38 to 65 = 27 years
- Average comp: ~$260K
- Annual contribution: ~$31K

## Total retirement portfolio at 65 from this plan

- Roth IRA: $1.4M (tax-free)
- HSA: $1.5M (tax-free if used for medical)
- SkyWest 401k from non-elective: $2.6M (taxed on withdrawal)
- **Total: ~$5.5M at age 65**

Plus whatever OSA produces, plus real estate equity, plus taxable savings from your high CA income.

## Why this is actually a strong plan

A few things worth noting before I push back on parts:

**The Roth IRA priority is correct.** At your age and income, Roth IRA is one of the most valuable accounts available. You're in a moderate tax bracket now, much higher later. Pay taxes now, never again. $1.4M of completely tax-free money at 65 is genuinely valuable.

**The HSA logic is correct and underutilized by most pilots.** The triple tax advantage (deductible going in, tax-free growth, tax-free for medical) is unmatched. Most people treat HSAs like checking accounts and miss the wealth-building potential. You're treating it as a long-term investment vehicle, which is the right move.

**The "I pay taxes" logic on Traditional 401k is partially right.** Traditional 401k saves taxes now but creates RMDs at 73 and forces you into ordinary income brackets in retirement. Many pilots end up with massive Traditional balances and complain about the tax bill. Roth and HSA avoid this entirely.

**Capturing the 12% non-elective passively is correct.** It's free money you can't refuse. No reason to overthink it.

## Where I'd push back on the plan

Three things to reconsider:

**Pushback 1: You're walking away from ~$30K-50K of guaranteed company match over the next 6 years**

This is the part of the plan where the "I pay taxes" logic doesn't fully apply. The company match isn't your money being taxed — it's *additional* compensation that only exists if you contribute. Every dollar you contribute up to the match cap is matched 1:1 by SkyWest.

At Year 4 benefits (4% tier) for ~$5K of your money: you get $5K match
At Year 5-9 benefits (6% tier) for ~$12K of your money: you get $12K match

Over 6 years (now to age 38), capturing match costs you ~$60K of contributions but adds ~$60K of company money you wouldn't otherwise see.

The tax argument: yes, Traditional 401k contributions reduce current taxes but create future taxable income. But here's the thing — **you can contribute to Roth 401k**. SkyWest's plan likely allows Roth 401k contributions. If you Roth-it, you pay taxes now (same as your stated preference), get the company match (which goes to Traditional bucket), and avoid future RMDs on your contributions.

Roth 401k contributions don't have the tax problem you're avoiding. The match goes to a Traditional bucket but that's the company's free money — taking it doesn't cost you anything.

**The $60K of captured match grows to ~$500K by age 65.** Walking away from $500K to avoid taxes that wouldn't apply to a Roth 401k contribution doesn't optimize.

**Pushback 2: HSA strategy needs verification**

HSAs require an HDHP (High Deductible Health Plan). Check that:
- SkyWest offers an HDHP option (most carriers do, but some pilots are on PPO)
- The HDHP makes sense for your family healthcare situation
- You're tracking your medical receipts so you can withdraw tax-free decades later

If you're on a PPO and switch to HDHP just for HSA access, run the math carefully — HDHPs have higher out-of-pocket costs that can offset HSA tax savings if you have significant healthcare needs.

**Pushback 3: The OSA + RE allocation deserves explicit modeling**

You're directing all post-tax discretionary income to OSA and real estate. Worth asking: what's the actual capital plan?

Right now you mentioned you're bootstrapping OSA. OSA's capital needs are likely modest because you're a solo founder with infrastructure costs ($25/month Supabase, S3, Lightsail). The big costs are time, not money. So how much capital actually goes to OSA per year? $20K? $50K? More?

For real estate in Chicago multifamily — those properties typically need $80K-150K down for a duplex/triplex in your target neighborhoods, plus reserves. That's a much bigger capital sink than OSA likely needs.

The question worth thinking through: are you sure you have enough capital to deploy that 401k contributions would actually compete with OSA/RE for funds? If your OSA capital need is $20K/year and your RE down payment timeline is 24-36 months out, you might have enough cash flow to do *both* — capture 4-6% match AND fund OSA AND save for RE down payment.

The math: at junior CA pay (~$190K), after-tax take-home is ~$130K. Living expenses for a family on $80K (very comfortable in Chicago suburbs) leaves $50K free annually. $12K to 401k match capture leaves $38K still available for OSA + RE. That might be enough.

## The honest tradeoff for you

This is the question to answer for yourself: **Is $9K/year of capital diverted from OSA + RE worth $1.1M of additional retirement wealth?**

Three ways to think about it:

**If OSA needs every dollar:** Skip the match, fund OSA. The plan you stated is internally consistent. You're betting OSA returns make up the $1.1M gap.

**If RE down payment is the binding constraint:** $9K/year is meaningful for accelerating a property purchase. If skipping match gets you into a Chicago duplex 12 months sooner, that property's appreciation + rental income may exceed the $1.1M lost retirement. Real estate compounds too.

**If neither OSA nor RE actually needs that exact $9K/year:** Capture the match. It's free money disguised as a retirement contribution.

## My honest read of your situation

Looking at OSA's bootstrap profile (low capex, mostly your time) and Chicago RE math (down payments 12-36 months out, not immediate), I don't think $9K/year is the binding constraint on either. You're earning $130K+ now, will earn $190K+ at upgrade in 12-18 months, and your living costs are reasonable.

The match capture in Roth 401k seems like the kind of thing that costs you almost nothing today and produces $500K+ at 65 in tax-free money. Combined with your stated Roth IRA and HSA strategy, you'd have approximately $3.6M of completely tax-free wealth at 65 ($1.4M Roth IRA + $1.5M HSA + $700K Roth 401k from match), which is genuinely impressive and rare among pilots.

But your call on this — you know OSA's capital needs better than I do, and "no debt to taxes" is a defensible philosophy even if it costs some absolute returns.

## What's actionable this week

Regardless of the match question, three things to do:

**1. Open Roth IRA if not already open.** Schwab, Fidelity, or Vanguard all work. Set up automatic monthly contributions of ~$583 ($7K/12). Start now.

## Phelipe

## Claude

Good question — this is the actual sophisticated tax strategy worth thinking through.

## How "skipping tax brackets" with Traditional 401k works

The mechanic: Traditional 401k contributions reduce your *current year* taxable income. If a contribution drops you from a higher marginal bracket into a lower one, you save the difference between brackets on those dollars.

Your 2026 federal tax brackets (married filing jointly assumed — adjust if single):

- 10% bracket: $0 - $23,850
- 12% bracket: $23,850 - $96,950
- **22% bracket: $96,950 - $206,700**
- **24% bracket: $206,700 - $394,600**
- **32% bracket: $394,600 - $501,050**
- 35% bracket: $501,050 - $751,600
- 37% bracket: $751,600+

Standard deduction MFJ 2026: ~$30,000

The key inflection points are at 22%→24%, 24%→32%, and 32%→35%. Those are the brackets where Traditional 401k "skipping" creates real value.

## Your projected income progression vs brackets

Let me map your career income against bracket thresholds:

**Now (age 32-33, FO Year 3-4):** ~$130K gross
- After standard deduction: ~$100K taxable
- Marginal rate: 22%
- Already mostly in 22% bracket; small portion at 12%
- **Traditional 401k saves 22% on contributions**

**Age 33-34 (upgrade transition):** ~$160K gross
- Taxable: ~$130K
- Marginal: 22%
- **Traditional saves 22%**

**Age 34-37 (junior CA, Year 5-7):** ~$190-220K gross
- Taxable: ~$160-190K
- Marginal: 22%, edges into 24%
- **Traditional saves 22-24%**

**Age 38-42 (mid CA, Year 10+ benefits, 12% non-elective starts):** ~$235-260K gross
- Taxable: ~$205-230K
- Marginal: 24% (just above 22% threshold)
- **Traditional saves 24%**

**Age 43-50 (senior CA, Year 15+):** ~$260-285K gross
- Taxable: ~$230-255K
- Marginal: 24%
- **Traditional saves 24%**

**Age 50-65 (top of scale, Year 22+):** ~$285-310K gross
- Taxable: ~$255-280K
- Marginal: 24%
- **Traditional saves 24%**

## The bracket reality for SkyWest pilots

Here's the inconvenient truth: **at SkyWest captain pay levels, you never hit the 32% bracket.** The 32% bracket starts at ~$395K for MFJ. Top of scale CA pay tops out around $310K gross. Even with full overtime, you're looking at $340-360K — still in the 24% bracket.

This means the bracket arbitrage opportunity is much smaller than it would be at a major airline where senior captains hit $450K+ and routinely operate in 32-35% brackets.

**Implication:** Your marginal rate basically stays at 22-24% your entire career at SkyWest. You don't get the dramatic 32%→24% skip that high-earning Delta or United captains use to justify Traditional contributions.

## Where Traditional 401k actually helps you

Even without major bracket skipping, Traditional has some value at certain points:

**Scenario 1: Years where OSA generates W2 or pass-through income on top of SkyWest pay**

If OSA distributes income to you (as owner draws or W2), your total income could push above $395K and into 32% bracket. Traditional 401k contributions against SkyWest pay could prevent that bracket creep.

This is the sleeper case worth planning for. If OSA hits even modest profitability and you take $100K+ out as personal income, suddenly you're at $400K+ combined and the bracket arbitrage matters.

**Scenario 2: Year of one-time income spike**

If you ever have a year with bonus income (OSA exit, RE sale gain, partnership distribution), Traditional 401k contributions reduce that year's taxable income disproportionately if it pushes you into a higher bracket.

**Scenario 3: Pre-retirement gap year**

The classic Traditional play: contribute Traditional during peak earning years (24% bracket), then in early retirement years (age 60-65 say) before Social Security and RMDs, your taxable income drops to $40-60K and you withdraw Traditional 401k at 12% bracket.

That's a 24% → 12% arbitrage, worth ~12% of contributed amount. Over $200K of Traditional contributions, that's ~$24K of savings.

## When the Traditional 401k math wins for you specifically

Three scenarios where I'd flip from "Roth 401k or skip" to "Traditional 401k":

**Scenario A: OSA generates additional income that pushes you into 32% bracket**

Trigger: Combined SkyWest + OSA income exceeds ~$395K MFJ taxable

If OSA hits this level, Traditional 401k contributions become very attractive — you save 32-35% on contributions, and even if you withdraw at 24% in retirement, the spread is meaningful.

Example: $24K Traditional contribution at 32% saves $7,680 in taxes. Withdraw later at 24% costs $5,760. Net savings: ~$1,920 per year. Over a decade, ~$25K of pure tax arbitrage.

**Scenario B: Real estate generates significant K-1 distributions on top of W2**

Trigger: Multifamily real estate produces $50K+ of distributable income

Same logic — combined income pushes you into higher brackets, Traditional 401k creates room to absorb the additional income at lower effective rates.

**Scenario C: You decide to retire/transition before 65**

Trigger: OSA exit funds early retirement at age 55-60

If you stop W2 income at 55, you have 5-10 years of low-bracket withdrawal years before SS and RMDs. Traditional 401k contributions made at 24% can be withdrawn at 12-22% during the gap, creating the classic deferred income arbitrage.

## What this means for your current strategy

Your stated plan (Roth IRA + HSA, skip 401k until 12% non-elective at 38) makes sense given:

1. You're currently at 22% marginal — not high enough to make Traditional dramatically valuable
2. Your career stays in 22-24% — limited bracket skipping opportunity
3. OSA capital deployment is the priority
4. The 12% non-elective is automatic and going to Traditional bucket regardless

The Traditional 401k decision really matters at one inflection point: **when OSA starts generating personal income that pushes your combined household income above $395K.**

That's the trigger to revisit. Until then, Roth IRA + HSA + automatic 12% non-elective is structurally efficient for your bracket reality.

## A specific framework for the future decision

When OSA starts producing personal income, here's the calculus:

**Decision rule:** If your projected combined household taxable income for the year will exceed $395K (MFJ) or $250K (single), max Traditional 401k contributions to bring it back below the 32% bracket threshold.

**Mechanic:** Start the year contributing Roth 401k. By Q3, project your year-end income. If trending above the bracket threshold, switch remaining contributions to Traditional. This lets you optimize bracket-by-bracket each year.

**Limit:** $23,500 personal contribution in 2026, growing roughly $500-1000/year. Catch-up adds $7,500 starting at 50.

## The broader tax picture worth considering

A few things that affect this beyond just 401k brackets:

**1. Roth IRA backdoor.** At single filer income above $165K (2026 phase-out), or MFJ above $246K, you can't contribute to Roth IRA directly. You'd need to do backdoor Roth (contribute to Traditional IRA, immediately convert to Roth). This becomes relevant at upgrade and beyond. Plan ahead so you don't have any pre-tax IRA money that complicates the conversion.

**2. State taxes.** Illinois flat 4.95% applies to all your earned income regardless. Traditional 401k saves Illinois tax now, paid back when withdrawn. If you ever moved to a no-state-tax state in retirement (FL, TX, TN, NV), Traditional becomes more attractive because you'd save IL tax now and pay zero state tax on withdrawal.

**3. Qualified Business Income (QBI) deduction.** If OSA is structured as pass-through (S-Corp or partnership), you may qualify for 20% QBI deduction on business income. This interacts with bracket calculations and can change Traditional 401k math.

**4. NIIT (Net Investment Income Tax).** 3.8% additional tax on investment income above $200K (single) or $250K (MFJ). Doesn't affect 401k directly but affects how you think about Roth vs Traditional in conjunction with taxable brokerage income.

## Concrete recommendation

For now (next 5-6 years until age 38):
- Roth IRA max: yes
- HSA max: yes
- 401k: 0% personal, take 12% non-elective at Year 10 automatically
- Direct excess capital to OSA + Chicago RE

When OSA produces personal income (probably Year 3-5 of OSA scaling, age 35-37):
- Re-evaluate. If combined household income trending toward $395K, start Traditional 401k contributions to manage brackets.
- Could be partial year switch (Roth until Q3, Traditional remainder) for granular bracket management.

If you ever exit OSA at significant capital gains:
- Year of exit, max Traditional contributions to absorb the income spike
- Following years, may have low W2 income that justifies Roth conversions of existing Traditional balance

The strategy isn't fixed — it adapts to your income reality each year. The trigger to flip into Traditional is income exceeding the 32% bracket threshold, not age or seniority.

Want me to model a specific OSA income scenario and show what the optimal 401k mix would look like in that year? Or run through the Roth IRA backdoor mechanics so you have that ready for when your income phases out direct Roth eligibility?

## Claude

That's interesting and actually changes the optimization significantly. Let me think through this honestly.

## How you stayed at 12% bracket

Your 2025 taxable income must have been under ~$96,950 MFJ or ~$48,475 single. That's possible at SkyWest FO pay if:

- You flew minimum guarantee (75 hours) most months
- Took unpaid leave or training periods
- Heavy use of pre-tax deductions (HSA, traditional 401k if any, FSA)
- Married filing jointly with non-working spouse, splitting brackets
- Significant business deductions if OSA is structured to capture losses against W2

At ~$130K gross with standard deduction (~$30K MFJ), you'd be at ~$100K taxable — right at the 22% threshold. To stay at 12%, you'd need taxable income under ~$97K, meaning gross of ~$127K or less, OR you found ways to shelter income.

Quick clarification question that affects the math significantly: are you filing married jointly or single? The bracket thresholds differ dramatically — MFJ 12% goes to ~$97K, single 12% only goes to ~$48K. If you're single and stayed at 12%, you flew very little. If MFJ, you have significant room.

## Tools to stay at 12% as your income rises

The 12% bracket is genuinely valuable to preserve. Long-term capital gains and qualified dividends are taxed at **0% if you stay in 12% bracket**. That's a huge benefit most people miss. So preserving 12% status isn't just about saving 10% on marginal income — it's about getting tax-free investment growth on top.

**Tool 1: Traditional 401k contributions**

Each dollar of Traditional 401k contribution reduces your taxable income by $1. If you're $20K above the 12% bracket threshold, $20K of Traditional 401k brings you back into 12%.

For you specifically — this is the cleanest answer to your question. Right now you said you're skipping 401k entirely. But Traditional 401k is the most powerful tool for managing bracket exactly because of this dynamic.

**Tool 2: HSA (you're already maxing — $4,400 self-only)**

Already deducted from gross income before federal tax calc. This is doing work for you.

**Tool 3: Pre-tax health insurance, FSA, commuter benefits**

Whatever SkyWest deducts pre-tax from your paycheck reduces your W2 box 1 income. Health insurance premiums, dependent care FSA ($5K limit), parking/transit FSA all help.

**Tool 4: OSA business expenses against business income**

If OSA has business income, OSA's expenses (servers, contractors, marketing, your home office, business meals, equipment) reduce OSA's taxable income. If OSA is structured as Schedule C or S-Corp, OSA losses can offset W2 in some scenarios (subject to passive activity rules and material participation tests).

**Tool 5: Qualified Business Income (QBI) deduction**

If OSA generates pass-through income, you get up to 20% deduction on that income. Doesn't reduce your bracket directly but reduces effective tax rate on OSA income.

**Tool 6: Real estate depreciation (Chicago RE play)**

This is the big one for your stated wealth strategy. Owning rental real estate generates "paper losses" through depreciation that can offset rental income and, if you qualify as a Real Estate Professional, can offset W2 income too.

A 4-unit Chicago multifamily at $600K with $480K depreciable basis generates ~$17K/year of depreciation. That's $17K of phantom losses against rental income. If structured properly with cost segregation studies, accelerated depreciation in early years can be much higher.

**Tool 7: Married filing jointly with income-shifting**

If MFJ, the bracket thresholds are double single filer. If your spouse has lower income or is non-working, you have significant room. If you can shift OSA income or RE income to spouse via partnership/LLC structuring, you can fill up their bracket headroom too.

## Your specific situation — what would actually keep you at 12%

Without knowing exact filing status and current taxable income, here's the framework:

**If you're MFJ at ~$130K gross now:**
- Standard deduction: $30K → taxable $100K
- $3K above 12% threshold ($97K)
- Need to shelter $3K to stay at 12%
- Currently sheltering: ~$4,400 HSA = below threshold automatically
- That's why you're at 12% with minimal effort

**If you're single at ~$130K gross:**
- Standard deduction: $15K → taxable $115K
- $66K above 12% threshold ($48K)
- Cannot realistically stay at 12% as single filer at this income
- Best you can do is 22% bracket optimization

**The critical question:** what's your filing status?

## Assuming MFJ, the path forward as income rises

This is where it gets interesting. Your income is about to jump significantly:

**At upgrade (next 12-18 months):** Captain pay ~$190K gross. Taxable: ~$160K. That's $63K above 12% threshold. To stay at 12%, you'd need to shelter $63K of income.

The math: $63K shelter requirement could come from:
- Max Traditional 401k: $23,500
- Max HSA: $4,400
- Max Traditional IRA (if eligible): $7,000 — but at this income, may not be deductible
- Real estate depreciation: $15-30K depending on properties owned

**Total achievable shelter: ~$50-60K** without aggressive structuring

You could realistically stay at 12% bracket through your first year of CA pay if you max Traditional 401k and own rental real estate generating depreciation. That's the strategy.

**At Year 5+ CA (mid-career, $230K+):** Taxable ~$200K. Need to shelter $103K to stay at 12%. That's harder — even maxed Traditional 401k + HSA + RE depreciation might only get you to ~$70-80K of shelter. You'd need significant RE portfolio (multiple properties) to make 12% achievable.

## The strategic shift this implies

If your goal is staying at 12% as long as possible, your current "skip 401k entirely" plan actively works against you. You're leaving the most powerful bracket-management tool unused.

**Revised approach if 12% bracket preservation is the priority:**

**Right now (age 32-33, FO pay):**
- Traditional 401k: contribute exactly enough to stay at 12% threshold
- Currently at 4% match tier — contributing 4% gives you ~$5,200 deduction + $5,200 match
- That likely keeps you firmly in 12% with margin
- Roth IRA: max $7K (Roth doesn't reduce current taxes but locks in 12% rate forever — perfect at low bracket)
- HSA: max $4,400 (you're doing this)

**At upgrade transition (age 33-34, taxable hits ~$130K):**
- Max Traditional 401k: $23,500 (drops taxable by $23.5K)
- HSA continues
- Roth IRA: still max $7K
- Net effect: stays in 12% bracket through most/all of first CA year

**Once consistently in 22-24% bracket (mid CA, age 36+):**
- Bracket preservation becomes harder
- Real estate becomes critical for continued shelter
- May need to accept 22% bracket and optimize within it

## The Roth IRA insight at 12% bracket

This is important and I want to highlight it: **at 12% federal bracket, Roth contributions are extraordinarily valuable.**

Why: you're paying 12% tax now, then growing tax-free forever. If you'd be in 22-24% bracket in retirement (likely with Traditional 401k withdrawals + Social Security), you're capturing a 10-12 percentage point spread on every Roth dollar.

$7,000 Roth IRA contribution at age 32 grows to ~$74,000 by age 65. Tax savings vs Traditional withdrawal at 24%: ~$18,000 per year. Compounded over 33 years of contributions, the Roth IRA priority during low-bracket years is worth hundreds of thousands.

You're already maxing Roth IRA. That's correct. Don't change that.

## What changes in your plan

The honest answer to your question "any way to keep at 12%": **yes, but you need to use Traditional 401k contributions strategically**, which is the opposite of your current "skip 401k" plan.

Here's the recalibrated strategy:

**For 2026 (before upgrade):**
- Contribute Traditional 401k to fill bracket headroom (probably 4-6% to capture match)
- Max Roth IRA (already planning)
- Max HSA (already planning)
- Net effect: stay at 12% bracket, capture company match

**At upgrade:**
- Traditional 401k contribution becomes your primary bracket-management tool
- Contribute as much Traditional as needed to stay at 12% — likely close to IRS max during transition year
- This is exactly the "tax bracket arbitrage" you were asking about

**Once mid-CA pay forces 22% bracket:**
- Switch back to Roth IRA priority
- Reduce or eliminate personal Traditional 401k (12% non-elective at Year 10 will be plenty of Traditional bucket)
- Build Roth bucket through Roth 401k contributions or backdoor Roth IRA

## The clean rule

Here's the framework: **maximize Traditional contributions when at 22%+ bracket. Maximize Roth contributions when at 12% bracket.**

Your situation is special because you're at the inflection. You're at 12% now, will be at 22-24% in 18 months, then potentially manageable back down with RE and Traditional 401k.

The strategic move: use this 12-18 month window of low-bracket status to load up Roth contributions (which you're doing). When upgrade hits, pivot to Traditional 401k specifically to manage bracket back down.

Three things to clarify before refining further:

2. **Current taxable income last year:** What was your 2025 W2 box 1 and what bracket did you actually file in? Helps calibrate where you sit.

## Claude

OK, single filer changes everything. Let me give you the real picture.

## Single filer 2026 federal tax brackets

- 10%: $0 - $11,925
- 12%: $11,925 - $48,475
- **22%: $48,475 - $103,350**
- **24%: $103,350 - $197,300**
- **32%: $197,300 - $250,525**
- 35%: $250,525 - $626,350
- 37%: $626,350+

Standard deduction single 2026: ~$15,000

## Your actual bracket situation

At ~$130K gross, single, with standard deduction:
- Taxable income: ~$115K
- You're firmly in 24% marginal bracket

If you stayed at "12%" — that's actually impossible for a single filer at SkyWest FO pay unless you filed an extremely low income year.

Two possibilities for what you meant:

**Possibility 1: Effective tax rate of 12%, not marginal bracket**
Effective rate is total tax paid divided by gross income. At ~$130K single:
- ~$11.9K at 10% = $1,193
- ~$36.5K at 12% = $4,386
- ~$54.9K at 22% = $12,084
- ~$11.7K at 24% = $2,820
- Total federal: ~$20,500
- Effective rate: ~16% on gross, ~18% on taxable

To get to 12% effective rate, you'd need taxable around $80K, meaning gross of ~$95K with significant deductions. Possible if you had heavy pre-tax deductions or partial-year employment.

**Possibility 2: You were comparing to last year's W2**
If you flew significantly less in 2024 (training year, transition, partial year hire), gross could have been $80-100K, putting you into actual 22% marginal with low effective rate.

## The reality of single filer brackets at SkyWest

Here's the hard truth for single filers:

**12% bracket ceiling: $48,475 taxable**

That's $63K gross with standard deduction. You can't realistically stay at 12% bracket on full SkyWest FO pay. The 12% ceiling for singles is too low — you're an FO pulling $115-150K gross typically.

**22% bracket ceiling: $103,350 taxable**

That's $118K gross with standard deduction. You could stay at 22% bracket marginal if you shelter aggressively. But on $130K+ gross, you'll edge into 24%.

**24% bracket ceiling: $197,300 taxable**

That's $212K gross. As a captain, you'll cross into 32% almost immediately on full flying.

**32% bracket starts at $197,300 taxable for singles** — much earlier than MFJ's $394K. This is the brutal part of single filer status. You hit 32% at junior CA pay. 35% at top of scale.

## Your projected single-filer brackets through your career

**Now (FO Year 3-4, ~$130K gross):**
- Taxable: ~$115K
- Marginal: 24%
- Effective: ~17%

**Upgrade (Year 4-5, ~$165K gross):**
- Taxable: ~$150K
- Marginal: 24%
- Effective: ~19%

**Junior CA (Year 5-7, ~$200K gross):**
- Taxable: ~$185K
- Marginal: 24% (right at edge)
- Effective: ~20%

**Mid CA (Year 8-12, ~$240K gross):**
- Taxable: ~$225K
- **Marginal: 32%** (crossed the threshold)
- Effective: ~22%

**Senior CA (Year 13+, ~$275-300K):**
- Taxable: ~$260-285K
- Marginal: 35%
- Effective: ~25%

This is why single filer SkyWest captains end up in much higher brackets than the MFJ math I was doing earlier. **You'll be in 32-35% bracket for most of your career as a captain.**

## What this means for your 401k strategy

This dramatically changes the Traditional vs Roth math.

**At 24% bracket (right now):** Roth still wins, but margin is thinner. Roth contributions cost you 24 cents per dollar in current tax. Worth it if retirement bracket is similar or higher.

**At 32% bracket (mid CA, age 38+):** Traditional starts winning hard. $24K Traditional contribution saves $7,680 in current taxes. Withdrawal at 22-24% in retirement (assuming income drops) costs $5,280-5,760. Net savings: $2,000-2,400 per year per max contribution. Over 15+ years, $30-40K of pure tax arbitrage.

**At 35% bracket (top of scale):** Traditional dominates. Same math, even larger spread.

## The strategic shift for single filer

Your stated plan needs revision because the bracket math is different:

**Now (24% marginal):**
- Roth IRA: Max $7K (still wins — locks in 24% rate, growth tax-free)
- HSA: Max $4,400 (always max — pre-tax going in helps even more at 24%)
- Traditional 401k: Consider 4-6% to capture match. Saves 24% tax now. The match itself is free money regardless.

**At upgrade (24% marginal):**
- Same as above, increased absolute amounts
- Match capture becomes more valuable as comp grows

**At Year 8+ (32% marginal):**
- **This is where Traditional 401k becomes very attractive**
- Max Traditional contributions ($23,500+)
- The 12% non-elective is already going to Traditional anyway
- Roth IRA backdoor (income too high for direct Roth)
- HSA continues as triple-tax-advantage

**At top of scale (35% marginal):**
- Max everything Traditional
- Backdoor Roth IRA still worthwhile for asset diversification
- Real estate depreciation becomes critical for managing AGI

## Can you stay in lower brackets?

The honest answer: not really, on SkyWest captain pay as a single filer. The brackets are too compressed.

What you can do is **manage the rate at which you cross brackets** through:

**Tool 1: Traditional 401k contributions**
- Max ($23,500 in 2026) reduces taxable income by that amount
- At mid CA pay, this can keep you in 24% rather than crossing into 32%
- Marginal impact: significant

**Tool 2: Real estate depreciation**
- Owning rental properties generates passive losses via depreciation
- 4-unit Chicago multifamily: ~$15-25K depre

## Claude

Now we have something concrete to optimize. Staying at 22% as a single filer — that's a real, achievable goal with the right structure.

## The 22% bracket ceiling for singles in 2026

**22% bracket cap: $103,350 taxable income**

That's the line you can't cross. Above that, you're in 24%.

To convert taxable income to gross income with standard deduction (~$15K):
- Gross of ~$118,350 = $103,350 taxable = right at 22% ceiling

So your maximum gross income while staying in 22% is roughly **$118,350** with no other deductions. With deductions (401k, HSA, etc.), you can have higher gross and still stay at 22%.

## Your shelter math

To stay at 22% on a given gross income, you need:

**Total deductions needed = Gross - $118,350**

At $130K gross now: need $11,650 of deductions beyond standard
At $165K gross (post-upgrade): need $46,650
At $200K gross (junior CA): need $81,650
At $240K gross (mid CA): need $121,650
At $300K gross (top of scale): need $181,650

The deduction toolkit available:

**Available shelter for single filer:**
- Traditional 401k: $23,500 (2026 limit)
- HSA: $4,400 (self-only)
- Traditional IRA: $7,000 (but deductibility phases out at high income with employer plan)
- Real estate depreciation: variable, $0-30K+ depending on properties

**Maximum standard shelter (no RE):** ~$28-35K depending on IRA deductibility

This is the constraint. Without real estate, you can shelter about $30K. Standard deduction adds another $15K. **Effective max gross to stay at 22% without RE: ~$148,350.**

## Bracket projection by career stage

**Now (age 32, FO Year 3-4, $130K gross):**
- Need to shelter: $11,650
- Available: $4,400 HSA + IRA partial + 401k = easily $30K+ available
- **Stays at 22%: yes, comfortably**

**At upgrade (age 33-34, $165K gross):**
- Need to shelter: $46,650
- Max 401k Traditional ($23,500) + HSA ($4,400) = $27,900
- **Gap: $18,750 — you cross into 24% marginal even maxing tax-deferred accounts**
- Real estate becomes the only way to stay at 22%

**Junior CA (age 34-37, $200K gross):**
- Need to shelter: $81,650
- 401k + HSA: $27,900
- Need additional $53,750 from RE depreciation or other sources
- **Requires significant RE portfolio (3-4 units worth) to stay at 22%**

**Mid CA + (age 38+, $240K+ gross):**
- Need to shelter: $121,650+
- Realistically not achievable without aggressive RE strategy or active RE professional status
- **Will be in 24% bracket regardless**

## The honest answer

**Staying at 22% bracket is achievable through your first year of CA pay if you maximize tax-deferred accounts.** Beyond that, single filer brackets are tight enough that you can't stay at 22% on captain pay without significant real estate offsets.

## Concrete strategy to maximize 22% bracket years

**Phase 1: Now through upgrade (age 32-34)**

This is the high-leverage window. You're naturally near the 22% ceiling, and modest sheltering keeps you firmly in 22%.

- **Traditional 401k: 4% to capture match** — saves 22% on $5,200 contribution, plus $5,200 free match. The 22% tax savings is $1,144 per year. The match is $5,200 of free money. Total benefit: ~$6,344/year.
- **HSA: max $4,400** — saves 22% federal + state, ~$1,200 in tax. Plus tax-free growth.
- **Roth IRA: max $7,000** — doesn't reduce current taxes but locks in 22% rate forever. Critical at low bracket.
- **Brokerage savings:** any leftover capital, taxable account for OSA + RE down payment fund.

**Bracket result:** Stays at 22% comfortably. You're sheltered well beyond what's needed.

**Phase 2: Upgrade year (age 33-34, transition)**

Income jumps mid-year. You'll have a partial year at FO pay and partial year at junior CA pay. This is your last clean 22% year without major RE strategy.

- **Traditional 401k: max $23,500** — saves 22% on full amount = $5,170 in tax savings, all going into retirement
- **HSA: max $4,400**
- **Roth IRA: max $7,000**
- **Total tax-deferred: $35,000** (plus standard deduction $15K = $50K total deduction)

**Bracket result:** Likely stays at 22% for the year. After this, the math gets harder.

**Phase 3: Junior CA onwards (age 34+, $190K+ gross)**

To stay at 22%, you need RE depreciation or you'll cross into 24%. The decision becomes:

- Accept 24% bracket for 4-6 years and optimize within it
- Aggressively pursue Chicago RE early to generate depreciation
- Some hybrid

## The Roth-Traditional shift point

This is the key insight for single filers:

**At 22% marginal:** Roth strongly preferred for IRA. Roth 401k or Traditional 401k roughly neutral, with slight Roth edge.

**At 24% marginal:** Traditional 401k starts winning marginally. Roth IRA still preferred via backdoor.

**At 32% marginal:** Traditional dominates aggressively.

For YOU specifically given your stated goal (stay at 22% as long as possible):

**Right now: contribute 4% Traditional 401k (capture match, stay at 22%).** Don't max it — you don't need to. Match capture is the high-value piece.

**At upgrade: max Traditional 401k.** Keeps you at 22% during transition year, captures full match.

**Junior CA Year 1: max Traditional 401k + start RE acquisition.** Try to stay at 22% with RE depreciation help.

**Mid CA forward: accept you're at 24% and optimize accordingly.**

## Why this strategy works

Three reasons this approach is good for you:

**1. Match capture at 22%** is essentially free. $5,200 of company match for $5,200 of your money, saving $1,144 in taxes. The contribution itself is post-tax neutral (you reduce taxable income by $5,200 but that money goes to retirement). The match and tax savings are pure gain.

**2. Roth IRA at 22% bracket** is exceptional value. You're paying low tax now, getting tax-free growth forever. Maximum value of Roth IRA happens at low brackets — exactly your situation.

**3. Bracket preservation is cheap right now, expensive later.** $5K of 401k contribution today keeps you at 22% with margin. Same $5K at junior CA pay barely moves the needle. Use the leverage while you have it.

## What to actually do this week

**Action 1: Set 401k contribution to 4% Traditional in Schwab portal.** This:
- Captures the 4% match (~$5,200/year free)
- Saves you ~$1,144/year in federal tax
- Keeps you firmly in 22% bracket
- Costs you ~$70/week in take-home pay

**Action 2: Confirm Roth IRA opened and funded.** Max $7,000 for 2026. If not contributed yet, can lump-sum or set up monthly $583 transfers.

**Action 3: Confirm HSA is on HDHP and invested.** Verify the funds above the cash threshold are in equity index funds, not sitting in money market.

**Total annual tax-advantaged contributions:** $35,400 ($23,500 401k future max + $4,400 HSA + $7,000 Roth IRA) — but right now you're contributing $5,200 + $4,400 + $7,000 = $16,600 personal, plus capturing $5,200 match.

## What changes at upgrade

When CA upgrade happens (probably mid-2027), reassess:

1. **Increase 401k contribution to maximize bracket management.** You'll need to contribute closer to max to stay at 22% during the transition year.

2. **Start RE acquisition planning seriously.** First Chicago multifamily purchase becomes your bracket-management tool for Year 5+ CA.

3. **Roth IRA may phase out at full CA pay.** Single filer Roth IRA phases out at $165K-$180K MAGI. At full CA pay you're above this. Need to do backdoor Roth.

4. **Continue HSA, maybe consider HSA family if spouse/dependents enter picture.**

## The match capture isn't optional anymore

Earlier you said you wanted to skip 401k entirely until age 38. With single filer 22% goal, that strategy actively hurts you. Here's why:

- Skipping match capture: lose $5,200/year of free money compounding for 33 years = ~$425K at 65
- Tax cost of contributing 4%: zero (you're saving 22% tax on each contribution)
- Cash flow impact: $70/week pre-tax, ~$55/week net after tax

You're not losing $5,200/year of OSA capital by capturing match. You're losing ~$3,800/year (the after-tax equivalent of your 4% contribution) and gaining $5,200 of free company money plus retirement growth.

The "I pay taxes" objection doesn't apply because:
- Roth 401k contributions can be made if you prefer (no future tax problem)
- Even Traditional 401k contributions at 22% bracket now will likely be withdrawn at lower or similar bracket in retirement gap years
- The match itself is the company's money — taking it doesn't create tax debt for you

## Bottom line answer

To stay at 22% bracket:

**Now:** Contribute 4% Traditional 401k (probably enough), max Roth IRA, max HSA. Easy.

**Through upgrade year:** Max Traditional 401k contributions. Last clean 22% year.

**Junior CA forward:** Buy Chicago multifamily real estate. Depreciation is the only way to stay at 22% on captain pay as a single filer. Without RE, accept you'll be at 24% from age 34 onward.

The good news: real estate is already part of your wealth strategy. The Chicago multifamily plan you've outlined isn't just for cash flow and equity — it's also your bracket management tool. The depreciation from rental real estate is what lets you stay in lower brackets while earning captain pay.

Want me to model what specific RE acquisitions would generate enough depreciation to keep you at 22% through different career stages? That would tell you the actual property count and timing needed.

Note: this assumes no 401k contributions (you said skip until 12% non-elective at Year 10), no Roth IRA, no HSA. If you maintain those, RE deployment is reduced by ~$15K/year. I'll model both scenarios.

## Cumulative capital available over career

**Pure RE deployment (no other tax-advantaged accounts):**

- Years 32-33 (2 years FO): $19K × 2 = $38K

## Chicago multifamily RE math

Let me use realistic Chicago numbers for your target neighborhoods (Avondale, Albany Park, Belmont Cragin, Portage Park).

**Typical 2-4 unit multifamily in those areas:**
- Purchase price: $450K-650K (current market, depends on condition and exact location)
- Down payment 25%: $112K-162K
- Closing costs: ~$15-20K
- Reserves required: ~$15-25K (lender requirement + capex buffer)
- **Total cash to close: ~$140K-200K per property**

Let's use $170K as average cash needed per property.

**Rental income (4-unit example):**
- 4 units × $1,500/mo average = $6,000/mo gross rent
- Vacancy + management (10%): -$600
- Property tax Cook County (residential 2-4 unit at 10% assessment): ~$10K/year = -$833/mo
- Insurance: -$250/mo
- Maintenance reserve (5%): -$300/mo
- Mortgage P&I on $480K at 7%: -$3,200/mo
- **Net cash flow: ~$800-900/mo = $10K/year**

This is realistic Chicago multifamily math. Cash flow is modest because you're buying for appreciation and depreciation tax benefits, not high cash-on-cash returns. Some properties will be better, some worse.

**Depreciation on property:**
- $600K purchase, ~$480K depreciable basis (excluding land)
- 27.5 year straight-line: ~$17,500/year of depreciation
- This shows as a "loss" on your taxes against rental income

- Close around age 42

**Property 5: age 44**
- Senior CA pay kicks in

After this, acquisition cadence speeds up because:

## Realistic portfolio trajectory

Conservatively assuming you buy a property every 18-24 months on average once cash flow stabilizes:

- Age 35: Property 1 (4-unit, $600K)
- Age 37: Property 2 (4-unit, $620K)
- Age 40: Property 3 (4-unit, $660K)
- Age 42: Property 4 (4-unit, $700K)

- Age 56: Property 10 (small apartment building, $2.2M)
- Age 60: Property 11 (small apartment building, $2.5M)
- Age 64: Property 12 (small apartment building, $2.8M)

Note: by age 50+ you're shifting to larger commercial multifamily because:
1. Better cash-on-cash returns at scale
2. Easier to manage (1 building vs 6)
3. Cook County assessment cliff (10% residential vs 25% commercial) — but commercial multifamily over 7 units gets assessed at 25%, so the math changes. Worth doing the calculation property-by-property.

## Portfolio value at 65

Working through this with realistic assumptions (3% annual appreciation, paying down mortgages on schedule, plus some refinancing for additional acquisitions):

**Property values at age 65:**
- Properties bought age 35-44 (held 21-30 years): mostly paid off, average value $1.0-1.4M each
- Properties bought age 46-50 (held 15-19 years): partially paid off, $1.5-2.0M each
- Properties bought age 53+ (held 12 years or less): less paid off but appreciated, $2.5-3.5M each

**Portfolio gross value at 65:** ~$22M-$28M
**Outstanding mortgages:** ~$8-12M
**Net equity:** ~$14M-$16M

**Annual cash flow at 65 (assuming portfolio fully optimized):**
- 12 properties averaging $15-20K/year cash flow each
- ~$180-240K/year passive income
- Plus continued appreciation
- Plus principal paydown by tenants

## The full picture at 65

**Real estate portfolio:**
- Net equity: ~$15M
- Annual cash flow: ~$200K
- Tax-advantaged through depreciation and 1031 exchanges

**SkyWest 12% non-elective (age 38-65):**
- ~$2.4M at 65 (taxable on withdrawal)

**Total wealth at 65: ~$17M-$18M**

Plus Social Security around age 67-70 if you take it.

## Important caveats and corrections

A few things to push back on in my own model:

**1. The $10K/year cash flow per property is conservative.** Actual experienced multifamily investors in Chicago target $15-25K/year per 4-unit through better deal selection, value-add improvements, and active management. Could shift portfolio outcomes 30-50% higher.

**2. The 3% appreciation assumption is conservative for Avondale/Logan Square area.** Those neighborhoods have appreciated 5-7% annually for the past decade. If that continues, portfolio value at 65 is meaningfully higher. If Chicago underperforms (which has happened in some periods), it could be lower.

**3. The Cook County assessment cliff is real and matters.** Properties stay residential rate (10% assessment) up to 6 units. At 7+ units they jump to 25% commercial. Your portfolio strategy should account for this. The shift to "small apartment buildings" I assumed at $1.2M+ would hit this cliff.

**4. Real estate management at scale becomes a job.** 12 properties needs property management infrastructure. PM fees of 8-10% of gross rent eat into returns. Or you spend significant time managing yourself, which conflicts with SkyWest schedule and personal life.

**5. I assumed you can keep getting mortgages.** After 4-5 properties, conventional Fannie/Freddie limits hit and you need commercial portfolio loans, which have higher rates and stricter terms. Refinances may or may not work depending on rate environment.

**6. Tax brackets aren't preserved without offset.** Buying RE with depreciation helps, but at $200K+ W2 income as captain, you can't fully offset that with passive losses unless you qualify as Real Estate Professional (need 750+ hours/year in RE activities, which is hard to combine with airline schedule).

## What I'd reconsider

Given the model results, three thoughts:

**Thought 1: The retirement number is huge but illiquid.** $15M of equity in Chicago multifamily is not the same as $15M in stocks. Selling triggers capital gains. You'd typically use it for cash flow ($200K/year passive) plus eventual 1031 exchanges. The wealth is real but managing it requires ongoing effort.

**Thought 2: The all-in RE strategy concentrates risk.** Chicago specifically — single metro market. Single asset class — multifamily residential. If Chicago has another period of decline (it's had several), your wealth concentrated there suffers. Some diversification (REITs in retirement accounts, taxable brokerage, geographic spread) reduces this.

**Thought 3: Skipping the 401k entirely costs more than the OSA framing suggested.** Earlier we established that capturing 4-6% match is essentially free money — $5-12K/year of contribution captures equal company money. Walking away from this for RE deployment might not be net positive because:
- Match capture: ~$50-80K of free money over career = ~$500K at 65 compounded
- Same $50-80K deployed to RE: maybe $200-400K of additional RE equity at 65

The 401k match capture might actually compound better than additional RE deployment, because match dollars grow tax-free in equity markets while additional RE dollars buy property at full cost.

## Modified strategy worth considering

Same RE-aggressive framework but with two tweaks:

**Tweak 1: Capture the match (4% now, 6% Year 5+)**
- Costs: ~$5-12K/year in pre-tax dollars
- After-tax cost: ~$3-9K/year less RE capital
- Trades $3-9K/year of RE capital for $5-12K/year of free money + tax savings + market growth

**Tweak 2: Max Roth IRA at 22% bracket years**
- $7K/year of post-tax contribution
- Reduces RE capital by $7K/year (post-tax)
- Builds tax-free retirement bucket of $1.4M at 65

**Tweak 3: Max HSA self**
- $4,400/year pre-tax
- Reduces RE capital by ~$3,400 after-tax
- Triple tax advantage, $700K at 65

**Total impact on RE deployment:** ~$15K/year less for RE, ~$2.6M of additional retirement diversification at 65

**Modified portfolio at 65:**
- RE equity: ~$13M (slightly less than $15M because $15K/year less deployment)
- 401k from match + 12% non-elective: ~$3M
- Roth IRA: $1.4M (tax-free)
- HSA: $700K (tax-free for medical)
- **Total: ~$18M with much better diversification and liquidity**

## Honest conclusion

Your "deploy everything above $70K to RE" strategy works mathematically. You'd build a $15M Chicago multifamily portfolio by 65 generating $200K/year passive income.

But pure RE concentration is risky and inefficient. Capturing the 401k match (free money), maxing Roth IRA (tax-free growth at low bracket), and maxing HSA (triple tax advantage) costs about $15K/year of RE capital but adds $2.6M of diversified retirement wealth. The marginal tradeoff strongly favors the diversified approach.

The wealth target is achievable. The strategic question is how concentrated you want it and whether you're willing to spend modest amounts on tax-advantaged accounts to reduce concentration risk.

## The honest range

**At 65, your RE portfolio is worth:**
- Conservative (3%): ~$19M total wealth
- Realistic (4.5%): ~$26M total wealth
- Aggressive (6%): ~$42M total wealth

That's a 2x range based purely on appreciation assumption. Appreciation is the dominant variable — way more important than cash flow optimization, slightly more important than mortgage paydown.

## Why appreciation drives everything

Look at Property 1 specifically:

Cash invested: ~$170K
Mortgage paid by tenants: $480K (pure equity gain over 30 years)
Appreciation at 4.5%: $1,650K (pure value increase over 30 years)

**Of total Property 1 wealth ($2.25M at 65):**
- $170K is your original cash (8%)
- $480K is mortgage paydown (21%)
- $1,650K is appreciation (74%)

Appreciation creates nearly 75% of the wealth. This is why neighborhood selection matters more than rent optimization. Avondale/Logan Square appreciating at 6% vs Belmont Cragin at 3% over 30 years on a $600K property is a $2M difference in outcome.

## What 4.5% really means

I want to push back on my own "realistic" assumption a bit. The 20-year Chicago citywide average of 3.5-4% includes:

- Major recession 2008-2012 (Chicago hit harder than coasts)
- Slower 2014-2018 period
- Boom 2019-2022
- Cooling 2023-2024

For your specific target neighborhoods (gentrifying, transit-accessible, near downtown):
- Avondale 10-year: ~6%
- Logan Square 10-year: ~6.5%
- Albany Park 10-year: ~5%
- Belmont Cragin 10-year: ~4%
- Portage Park 10-year: ~4.5%

If you concentrate in Avondale/Logan Square style neighborhoods, 5-6% is more defensible than 4.5% as a 30-year forward assumption. But 30 years is long, and reversion to mean is real. 4-5% feels right as a planning assumption.

## Two scenarios that change the math

**Scenario A: Chicago stagnates**

If Chicago has another decade like 2008-2018 (slow appreciation, population outflow), your portfolio could realistically only do 2-3% annually. That drops total wealth to ~$15-18M.

Risks:
- Illinois fiscal problems → property tax increases reduce returns
- Population outflow continues
- Crime/policy issues drive demand to suburbs

**Scenario B: Chicago booms**

If Chicago becomes a real winner in the next 30 years (corporate HQs, tech growth, urban revitalization), 6-7% appreciation is achievable. Portfolio could be $40M+.

Catalysts:
- Major corporate relocations
- Climate migration favoring Midwest
- Federal infrastructure investment
- Continued gentrification

## Cash flow appreciation also matters

Rents grow over time too. I assumed flat rent in my cash flow numbers, which isn't right.

**Rent inflation (Chicago 30-year average): ~2.5-3% annually**

Property 1 cash flow trajectory:
- Age 35 (Year 1): $10K/year
- Age 45 (Year 10 with 3% rent growth): $13.4K/year
- Age 55 (Year 20): $18.0K/year
- Age 65 (Year 30): $24.3K/year

By the time you're 65, the original Property 1 generates $24K/year in current dollars, equivalent to $10-12K in age-35 dollars. So rent inflation roughly keeps pace with general inflation, preserving real cash flow.

But here's the kicker: once mortgages are paid off, cash flow jumps significantly because there's no more P&I payment. Property 1 with no mortgage at age 65 generates roughly $40-50K/year of cash flow vs. $24K with mortgage.

**Total portfolio cash flow at 65 (realistic scenario):**
- 6 properties fully paid off: ~$45K each = $270K/year
- 6 properties partially mortgaged: ~$25K each = $150K/year
- **Total annual cash flow: ~$420K/year**

That's the passive income picture. $420K/year of rental income at 65 is more than your peak SkyWest captain salary, completely passive.

## The real number for retirement planning

Combining everything at age 65 (realistic scenario, 4.5% appreciation):

**Net worth:**
- RE equity: $21.7M
- Accumulated cash flow saved/invested: ~$4M
- SkyWest 401k 12% non-elective: $2.4M
- **Total: ~$28M**

**Annual passive income at 65:**
- RE cash flow: $420K
- 401k 4% safe withdrawal: $96K
- **Total: ~$516K/year**

This is substantially more than my first projection because appreciation compounds dramatically over 30 years on a portfolio you keep adding to.

## Sanity check on assumptions

Two things that could break this model:

**1. Refinancing assumptions**

I'm assuming you can keep getting mortgages on properties 5-12. In reality:
- Conventional Fannie/Freddie limit: 10 financed properties (sometimes 4 in practice)
- After that, commercial portfolio loans at higher rates
- Cash-out refinances during low-rate periods help fund acquisitions
- Higher rate environment (current 7%) makes deals tighter than 4% era

The portfolio plan assumes you successfully navigate the financing escalation. Realistic but not guaranteed.

**2. Time and management capacity**

12 properties = roughly 36-50 units depending on mix. That's a job. Property management at 8-10% of gross rent eats into returns but is necessary at scale. The model assumes effective management — could be PM company, could be partners, could be your own time investment. Either way, it's a real constraint.

**3. Capital expenditure shocks**

Old Chicago multifamily has periodic major capex events: roofs ($30-50K), tuckpointing ($15-30K), HVAC system replacements ($10-20K per unit), plumbing repairs in 100-year-old buildings can be substantial. Reserves of 5-8% of gross rent should cover this on average but lumpy years can be painful.

## Bottom line

**Your portfolio at 65 (realistic 4.5% appreciation):**
- Net worth: ~$26M
- Annual passive income: ~$420K
- Tax-advantaged through depreciation, 1031 exchanges, step-up basis at death

The appreciation is the engine. Mortgage paydown is meaningful. Cash flow funds the next acquisitions and your living expenses post-retirement. All three combine to create a multi-million-dollar wealth machine that's mostly passive once built.

The strategy works. The execution risks are real (financing, management, market conditions, capex). The wealth ceiling under good conditions is significantly higher than you'd build through any combination of 401k + Roth + HSA + brokerage.

Want me to model what happens if you also do the small tax-advantaged contributions (~$15K/year) alongside this RE strategy, or what 1031 exchanges in your 50s could do to optimize the portfolio further?
